None survives being reduced to "reply YES", so the SMS path for a caregiver is a **link to the flow that asks properly** — which is also the estimate's own words, "the same structured capture as the Seed Tool", read literally. Refusing to collect something badly is not a missing feature. **The link is `/share`, not `/caregiver`** — the latter is where a caregiver signs *themselves* up (2C), and sending a nominating parent there would be the wrong flow entirely. Caregiver words are matched as a **substring**, unlike every other parser here, and the asymmetry is deliberate: a false positive costs a parent a link to a form that can take their nomination properly, a false negative builds a caregiver record by a path that cannot ask the 18+ question. **Five questions, not eleven**, because over SMS each one is a round trip that may take a day: name, kind, firsthand, recommendation, and anything another parent should know. Everything a trust label rests on is in that list (`trust-labels.ts` reads exactly `firsthand` and `recommendation`); the rest is an admin's follow-up rather than a fifth text. **Three things are never taken from the parent's texts:** the neighborhood is read from their profile (the 11 Aug rule that the graph is derived server-side), the status is `pending_review` on both rows, and `provenance` is `parent_submitted`. **The trap worth remembering:** the first version offered `camp` as a value, and `share_kind` has four members with camp not among them — camps are a first-class *taxonomy* category (§8.4/§15.3) and have never been a share kind. It would have failed on the enum at runtime with a clean typecheck and green tests: the `bands` shape of bug, caught by reading the enum rather than trusting the word. A camp is now a word the parent may type that lands where every other camp already is. **Two constraints do the state-keeping rather than code:** one open capture per person (two would make the next reply ambiguous, which is the failure one-question-at-a-time exists to prevent) and `status = 'open'` ⇔ `step is not null` (without it a finished capture keeps swallowing every later message). Both walked live. **And the capture is checked *before* the blast attachment**, on the settings-command reasoning: it is a conversation the parent started, and the message in front of us answers the question Pando asked one text ago. |

(10.3) — and **nothing in the message separates them.** The first version resolved it by which `if` came first, which is the same class of mistake as 8.3's bare "5" meaning a month or a five-year-old: it would silently refresh a record when the parent was grading an answer, and both writes are invisible to whoever made the error. So both pendings are fetched and **the more recently asked question wins**, which is the mechanism the rest of the inbound path already uses — the records decide, never the code order. | **The ladder counts what somebody did; being used is a receipt, not a rung** (1 Sep) | 9.4, and the weight that looks like an omission is the design. `answer_used` is worth **zero** toward a tier. It is the best signal that a contributor genuinely helped — 9.3's stated purpose — but it is an *outcome* rather than an act, and it is unbounded: one popular recommendation used fifty times would mint a Local Expert out of a single share, while a parent who answers ten questions about quieter subjects stays a Contributor. So the ladder counts acts and the grove reports outcomes; both read the same table and only one is a threshold. **Four more rules.** (1) *Founding is checked first and is permanent* — it is granted by a person on the second approved contribution, and the ledger started in September while the seed cohort's contributions were approved in August, so a volume check running first would report every founding contributor as a Member on day one. (2) *The ladder is lifetime and monotonic.* A rolling window would make somebody a Local Expert in March and a Contributor in May having done nothing wrong; access earned is not access rented. Current engagement already has a mechanism — the response-rate governor lowers how often a quiet contributor is **asked** — and duplicating it here would punish the same silence twice. (3) *Three freshness confirmations are one quality response*, which is 9.4's own number and its own word "configurable", so it is `FRESHNESS_PER_RESPONSE` rather than a `/ 3` in a query; the remainder is kept rather than rounded, or somebody confirming twice a month would sit at zero forever. An **unrated** blast answer is worth half — answering is giving, but until an admin has rated it nobody has said it was a quality response. (4) *No score is ever shown to a contributor.* 9.4 says the reward is better access rather than points and strategy §13 says no leaderboard ever, so `nextTier` exists for the admin's page and the parent sees a status. **What is deliberately absent:** the entitlements. §13 posts exchange rates for the grove (5 leaves = a free Targeted Ask, 15 = a month, 40 = a year) and that ledger is in no estimate row and is not quoted — inventing a price here would be the app granting money nobody agreed to. **And there is no tier column**: `tierFor` computes a standing from the events every time, so 9.5's "tier recalculation" job is deliberately a *ledger* sweep instead — a nightly write of a tier into a column would only create the second copy that goes stale, the same reason `matching.ts` recomputes an age band. |
| **A thank-you is earned by a parent's yes, and a silence is not a no** (1 Sep) | 9.1 and 9.2, which look like one loop and are two: 9.1 asks the parent who *received* an answer whether it helped; 9.2 thanks the contributors whose recommendation it was. The join between them did not exist — an answer knew what it said and never which records it said it from — so `drizzle/0026` adds `answers.share_ids`, without which there is no path from "we booked it" back to the person who earned the thanks. **Five rules.** (1) *A silence is not a verdict.* `helped` is null until they reply, and null never means no: it neither blames a recommendation nor mints a thank-you, and recording an unanswered prompt as a failure would be the system inventing evidence. (2) *The window has two bounds and both do work.* The floor (3 days for an activity, 7 for a caregiver) is because a parent asked the evening they got the answer has not been anywhere yet; the **ceiling** (5 and 14) is because past it the answer is a memory, and a stale yes is worse than none — it enters the ledger as though somebody had just used the recommendation. An answer past its ceiling is never asked about, so there is exactly one chance, which is why `helped_asked_at` is stamped **after** the send and not before. (3) *The slowest kind wins.* One answer naming a class and a sitter is asked about on the sitter's clock: 9.1 asks one question, and asking on the class's would catch the parent before they had done the slower half. (4) *The reply parser refuses to guess.* Exact on the whole message — "no idea, we never got round to it" and "yes we're still deciding" both return null and fall through to ordinary handling, because a yes writes an impact event **and texts a third person**, so a wrong reading reaches somebody who was never in the conversation. `YES` is safe to ask for only because it was deliberately removed from the opt-in keywords (3 Aug). (5) *One thank-you per contributor per week, and a held batch is owed rather than dropped.* Three messages is not three times the gratitude, it is Pando being noisy at the person being helpful. The week is measured from `message_log`, which `sendSms` writes only after the provider accepted — so a refused send leaves the batch owed. **The copy names what they did** (§13's impact receipts: a bare "thanks for contributing" is a form letter) and **asks for nothing** — a thank-you carrying a request is a request wearing a thank-you, and it would spend a message from an allowance the contributor agreed to for being *asked things*. ⚠️ **Both strings need checking against the registered A2P sample**, and this one is named: "QC Answers + A2P Prep" Part 2 lists a thanks among the three registered samples. |
| **Delivery health leads with the instruction, not the percentage** (27 Aug) | `/admin/delivery`, the last of M12. The page answers three questions in order — is anything wrong *now*, how bad, and how much is not yet known — and the order is the design. **The alerts sit above the rate** because two of the three carry an instruction rather than a number (30034 says stop sending; 21610 says our own suppression failed), and a reader who met the percentage first would already have decided how worried to be. **In-flight messages are shown as their own figure**, never folded into the rate: a message Twilio has accepted and not reported on is neither delivered nor failed, and counting it either way makes the number move with when the page was opened. **A null rate is stated as "not a failure"** rather than rendered as 0%, the same honesty rule as `persisted: false`. **No nav count**, like the matching harness: the sidebar's numbers mean something is waiting for you, and a rate is a gauge. And the page carries the one diagnosis its own emptiness can mean — if messages are going out and nothing appears here, the Messaging Service has no status callback pointing at `/api/sms/status`, so the sends are real and only the statuses never come back. |
| **A send is not a delivery, and 21610 is our bug** (27 Aug) | 12.5 and 13.4. `message_log` recorded that Pando sent something; whether it *arrived* comes back later on a separate callback, so `drizzle/0020` adds `status`, `error_code` and `status_at` and `/api/sms/status` fills them. **Four rules.** (1) *The three errors are kept apart because each has a different answer* — 30034 means every send is failing and the answer is to **stop**, not retry; 30007 means a carrier read the wording as spam and the copy needs review (and it is registered text, so a change means re-registering the sample); **21610 means Pando texted somebody who had opted out, which is a failure of our own suppression rather than carrier noise.** One "delivery error" bucket would bury the only one that is a bug in Pando. An unknown code is still surfaced — a silent one is how a new failure mode goes unnoticed for a week. (2) *In-flight messages are excluded from the rate, not counted as failures.* A message Twilio has accepted and not yet reported on is neither, and counting it either way makes the number swing with how recently somebody opened the page. (3) *A terminal status is never overwritten by an earlier one* — callbacks arrive out of order, and a late `sent` landing on a `delivered` would quietly lower the rate for a message that arrived. (4) *The status callback is signed like the inbound one*, because it writes to the table both the protection rules and the health page read: a forged callback could mark every failure as delivered and hide the exact outage 12.5 exists to surface. |
| **The acceptance checks run against the live database, and one of them caught the test itself** (27 Aug) | 12.6, and it is the five the estimate names — STOP silences every message type including scheduled jobs; STOP then START resumes with a new consent timestamp; nothing proactive leaves outside 8am–9pm PT, forced at a blocked hour; opted-out contributors never appear in a pool; and **a consent record exists for everyone who ever received a proactive message**. They are asserted at the layer that would actually be audited — the queries and the constants — rather than by mocking the send layer and trusting it calls them. **The fifth one failed on its first real run**, reporting two people messaged without consent. They were rows from this file's own earlier crashed runs, left behind because the failure happened before cleanup — so the check was right and the harness was wrong. Two consequences kept: cleanup now also removes what an earlier crashed run left, or one bad run poisons every later one; and it is **scoped to `is_test`**, because a name-prefix match alone would delete a real contributor called Cleanthes, and a test that can delete a real person is worse than one that leaves rows behind. |
| **A keyword is handled before the AI, and an unsigned webhook is not a request** (27 Aug) | 13.2 and 12.3/12.4. **The signature is not hardening to add later.** The webhook URL is public and everything behind it acts on what the request claims, so a forged POST could opt somebody out, opt somebody back *in* after they asked to stop, or write a fake reply that raises a contributor's response rate and keeps Pando texting them. Three details do the damage and each has a test: the signed URL must be the one Twilio called **byte for byte** and comes from `TWILIO_WEBHOOK_BASE_URL` rather than a host header (behind Traefik that header is whatever reached the container); the payload is `url + sorted(key + value)` with no separators; and the comparison is `timingSafeEqual` after a length check, because `timingSafeEqual` throws on a length mismatch and that throw is itself a timing signal. **Unconfigured refuses** — an endpoint that skips verification when a secret is missing is unauthenticated the moment somebody mis-deploys, and silently. **The keyword runs before anything else**, which is 5.3's own requirement ("opt-out keywords are handled before this step and never reach the AI"): a classifier is probabilistic and will one day read STOP as conversation, which is a compliance failure rather than a bad answer. **Matching is exact on the whole message**, so "stop by the park at 3" is not an opt-out — a substring test would silence people who never asked to be silenced, and the carrier layer catches a real STOP anyway. **YES is still not an opt-in** (START and UNSTOP only), because a parent answering "yes" to a Network Ask must never read as a re-subscribe. Two smaller rules: **no reply to STOP** — Twilio's Advanced Opt-Out sends the carrier-standard confirmation, and a second message is two goodbyes to somebody who asked for silence; and **an inbound is linked to the outreach it answers**, which is the entire mechanism behind 8.4's response rate. SMS carries no thread id, so the rule is the honest approximation — the most recent proactive message within the window — and **a keyword is never a reply to anything**, because STOP is a decision about Pando rather than an answer to a question. A cold inbound from an unknown number is logged with a null person rather than dropped: that arrival is 5.9's whole subject. |
| **The tiers are the strategy's four, not the estimate's five — and a Targeted Ask reaches five people, not twenty-five** (27 Aug) | 7.2, and the two documents disagree by more than naming. Estimate 7.2 lists passive · standard (~20) · targeted (~25) · precision (~15) · last-minute (~35). Strategy §8 lists **Passive (free) · Board Ask $5 · Targeted Ask $15 · Last-Minute Care (free in the pilot)**, and says outright that human review of an unusual match "absorbed what we once called Precision". The newer client document wins — this file's own rule, and the same call already taken for the five-day gap. **The pool size is the substance, not the names.** The estimate's core paid tier reaches ~25 people; the strategy's reaches "three to five carefully matched parents", and §6 gives the reason in one sentence: *"Pando never sends a question to everyone — that's how group chats train people to ignore things."* Twenty-five is a broadcast, five is a request, and the strategy's whole claim is that being asked should feel like a compliment. **Worth putting to the client before the first paid Ask**, because it changes what $15 buys — the numbers live in one file so the change is an edit. **Three things that follow.** (1) *Precision is a condition Pando detects, never a tier a parent buys*: `needsHumanReview` fires on the tier (Last-Minute Care always), on a **short pool** — sending to two while charging for five is what the guarantee exists to prevent — and on **stacked requirements**, which is where a scorer is confident and wrong. (2) *A passive entry contacts nobody, enforced by a CHECK*, because it is 7.11's demand map rather than an outreach. (3) *The refund test is an **approved** answer, not a reply*: "no idea, sorry" must not discharge a guarantee that promised a useful answer. |
| **An inbound text is proof of possession, and its consent says so** (27 Aug) | 5.9's repo half, and it turns on a reading of invariant 11. That invariant is that **nothing about a named parent is stored before their phone is verified**, and its purpose is that a stored profile belongs to somebody who actually holds the number. **An inbound SMS is exactly that proof** — the carrier delivered it from that number, which is the same fact an OTP establishes with more ceremony — so `phone_verified_at` is set from the webhook, and it remains "a server fact read from the verification, never a field the client can set": the server here is the signed Twilio webhook. **Two things keep it from being a loophole.** (1) *No name is stored.* The row has a phone and nothing identifying, which is precisely why the `verified_if_named` CHECK permits it — a name still requires the ordinary flow. (2) *The consent records what happened, not what they read.* A cold parent never saw `SMS_CONSENT_TEXT`; recording their opt-in under the seed wording's version would claim they read a paragraph they were never shown, on the one record a TCPA complaint actually tests. `INBOUND_CONSENT_VERSION` says "inbound text", and the defence is the message itself. Verified live that 12.6's "a consent record exists for everyone who received a proactive message" is satisfied by this path. **Three smaller rules.** The clarifying answer is checked **after** the blast attachment, because somebody answering a Network Ask is not also answering last week's question. A reply that cannot be parsed stores nothing **and is not asked again** — one refusal is a parent who did not want to answer, and asking twice is how a helpful service becomes a form. And the age writes a `children` row with **no `age_range` edge**: `matching.ts` recomputes the band from `birth_year` at query time precisely because a stored band goes stale, so writing one here would create the second copy that decision exists to avoid. |
| **A cold inbound is answered first and asked one thing, and the parser refuses to guess** (27 Aug) | 5.9 and 5.4. **The order is the product.** 5.9 says a stranger is "answered immediately with clearly-labeled public information — **never gated** — and the label itself explains why a profile is worth having". They usually arrived from a forwarded answer, so the next message is their entire impression of Pando, and a form would be the wrong one. The tap-through profile is not asked for until they try to use their free Ask, "framed as an unlock rather than a restriction". **One question at a time, always** — two in a text get one answer and then Pando has to guess which. Age first, because it changes what the answer should even contain; then the area. Only those two, because they are what `matching.ts` needs before it can rank anybody at all — everything else the seed profile collects makes matching *better*, these make it possible. **What Pando asked is remembered in `message_log.template`** (`clarify_child_age`), not in a new table: the last outbound clarify template *is* the pending question, and the inbound that names it is the answer. **The parsing refuses to guess, and three of its rules came from failing tests.** (1) The age boundary is at the **start only** with a lookahead for another digit — a trailing `` rejects "11yo", which is an ordinary answer, while a missing lookahead reads "2021" as twenty. (2) A neighborhood matches on the **first two slug parts as three-letter prefixes**: requiring every part fails "la canada" (a parent who dropped "flintridge" and is still unambiguous), and a four-letter prefix fails "south pas" (how somebody actually types it). | **Approving an answer and sending it are two buttons** (27 Aug) | 14.2. They are different events: approving is a judgement — durable, audited, final — while sending is a delivery attempt that can fail on a carrier and be retried without anybody re-approving anything. One button would mean a hiccup either lost the decision or wrote a second audit row for it. It also keeps the audit transaction off the network: `answer.send` does hold a connection across one HTTP call to Twilio, which is acceptable at pilot volume and noted in the code — `sendSms` never throws, so a refused send cannot roll back the audit row for a decision that was made. **The state this creates is called out rather than filed under done:** an approved answer nobody sent is a parent still waiting, so it has its own gold card on the page. **What the reviewer is actually checking is the claim, not the prose.** Every answer shows the trust labels it rests on as their own row — "Validated by multiple parents" is true on a record two parents used and is the single most damaging thing Pando could say on one that one parent used — and a label spotted inside a paragraph is a label nobody checks. The labels are **not editable**: they describe the records, so disagreeing with one is a fix in the contributions queue. **Two smaller things.** The hold reason is worded to keep "held because everything is" apart from "held because of what is in it" — `labels.ts` reads the blanket rule as a routine and the specific ones as reasons. And an answer to somebody with no profile is marked "New number", because that is 5.9's cold inbound and the reply is their entire first impression of Pando. |
| **The text that is sent is the text that was read, and the pilot reads everything** (27 Aug) | 5.8 and 14.2. **Two layers, and they are not the same rule.** `PILOT_HOLD_EVERYTHING` is the blanket one — strategy §19, *"for the first months every contribution is read by a person, because that is how we learn what 'good' looks like well enough to safely automate any of it"* — and it is a **named constant rather than a hard-coded true**, because it is meant to come off and the specific rules must already be right on that day. The specific rules are written and tested now, while getting them wrong is cheap: sensitive and caregiver-related and "the generator asked" are **permanent** holds (§19 keeps human eyes on those forever), while public-only and thin are temporary — a four-parent answer is exactly the ordinary recommendation §19 says can eventually pass. **The reason is carried, not just the verdict**, and the specific reason beats the blanket one: a queue that cannot say why a row is in it gets worked in arrival order, and an answer held only because everything is held reads differently from one that will always wait. **The rule the table exists for:** `answer_text` is stored when the answer is queued and sent **verbatim** on approval, never recomposed. Recomposing at send time would let the records move in between — a contribution approved, a record gone stale — and the parent would receive something nobody read; if the stored text is out of date the answer is rejected and a new one composed, which is a decision rather than a drift. **Two smaller ones.** `status` becomes `sent` **only when the send layer says it went** — a row marked sent for a message the carrier refused is the same lie as `persisted: true` on a failed write, and here it would also hide the commonest refusal, which is somebody who opted out. And **the labels are not editable**: they are the product's claim about where the answer came from, so an admin who disagrees with one disagrees with the records, and that is a fix in the contributions queue rather than a rewrite here. |

None of that shows up in a test of the model — it shows up as a parent acting on something Pando never knew. So the composition is arithmetic over records, and the only thing a model could usefully add later is **tone, under a constraint that it may not introduce a fact**. That layer is deliberately absent. **Five rules in the composer.** (1) *The opening counts only parent-backed records* — the 5.6 guard arriving in the prose, where it is easiest to lose: one public record beside one parent's reads as "One local parent", never two. (2) *Evidence outranks freshness.* Three parents on a stale record beat one on a fresh one, because the spec's answer to old knowledge is to **mark** it — and the marking is in the words ("this one is old, so treat it as a starting point"). A fresh record says nothing about its age, since saying "fresh" out loud is noise. (3) *`answer_ready` outranks the count*, because it is a human having already looked. (4) *The length budget drops whole records and never truncates* — an answer ending in "recommended by three par" is worse than one mentioning two places — and the budget is checked **with the forwardable line included**, not after it is appended. (5) *A solid answer does not offer a Network Ask*: offering when Pando already answered is selling something the parent does not need, and not offering when the answer is thin leaves them without the thing that would help. **One shape worth keeping:** `claimsAParent` is *given* the labels rather than importing them, because a value import would make this module unloadable in a plain node test — the `matching.ts` lesson — while copying the strings would put approved copy in two places. That signal comes from the records, costs nothing, and is checked **before** any model call. (2) *`unclear` and `chitchat` are different answers.* One is a confident reading of a harmless message; the other is the absence of a reading, routed to a person. Collapsing them would make every failure look like small talk and drop real questions on the floor. (3) *An intent the model invented falls back*, however confident it was — a high score on a value that does not exist is still nonsense. The floor is **0.6**, the same number the extraction pass and the admin's low-confidence filter already use, because an admin who has learned what "low confidence" means should not have to learn a second one. **The model is deliberately not wired into the webhook yet.** Nothing consumes the answer — 5.7 is the consumer and it does not exist — so an API call per inbound message would be money spent on a log line. (2) *`firsthand` is **false**.* A text message never establishes that the writer used the thing themselves; the seed flow asks that explicitly (R1) and a reply does not, so assuming yes would manufacture the one signal every trust label rests on. The row goes into `job_runs` **first**, and a partial unique index on `(job) where finished_at is null` turns that into a lock: a second attempt while one is in flight fails on the constraint rather than racing it. A cron that fires twice, a container restarted mid-run and two hosts briefly overlapping are all the same event to the database — and the expensive version of getting it wrong is `freshness_ping` texting the same parent twice. **A crashed run blocks, deliberately.** An old row with no `finished_at` keeps the job from starting, because a job that quietly unblocks itself after a crash hides the crash; clearing it is a decision somebody makes. **The two refusals are kept apart:** `already_running` is a concurrency event and `too_soon` is a misconfigured schedule, and they want different responses. **Which jobs touch a phone is on the spec, not in the body** — `sends: true` on exactly one of the three, because a read-only job run twice wastes a query while a sending one run twice is two texts to somebody who agreed to five a month. **A job assumes nothing about the rules:** `freshness_ping` decides only who is worth asking, and `sendSms` re-runs opt-out, quiet hours, the five-day gap, the ceiling and one-ping-a-month — a refusal counts as a **skip**, not a failure, because a contributor inside their gap is the system working. **The endpoint answers 200 on a refusal**, so a five-minute cron does not alert on the ordinary case; and `JOBS_SECRET` unset means refused, the same fail-closed rule as the Twilio signature, because these jobs send texts and grant credits. |

Both need a **real `message_log` id**. | **The send layer fails closed, and a missing person is a refusal** (27 Aug) | 12.1 wires invariant 6 for real: opt-out → quiet hours → contributor protection → provider. **Four rules that are not the app's usual ones.** (1) *Every function in `repo/outreach.ts` refuses when the database is unreachable* — the exact opposite of `persisted: false`, and deliberately: elsewhere an outage means "we could not save your answer", which is recoverable, while here it would mean "we could not check whether she asked us to stop", and sending anyway cannot be taken back. (2) *An outreach send without a `personId` is refused, not sent unchecked.* The limits are per person, so a caller that cannot say who this is for must not be able to route around them — a missing id is the obvious accidental bypass. (3) *Quiet hours exempt a live reply, and `inReplyTo` is separate from `transactional` for that reason* — 12.1's own wording is "direct replies in a live conversation are exempt", and answering a parent who texted at 10pm is a reply rather than an intrusion. (4) *`message_log` is written **after** the provider accepts.* Every later rule reads that table — the monthly cap, the five-day gap, the governor, and 12.5's delivery monitoring — so a row written before the send would spend somebody's allowance on a message that never went. The write is deliberately not allowed to fail the send: the text has already gone, and reporting a failure would produce a duplicate. **The numbers.** The gap is **five days** in one constant (`OUTREACH_GAP_DAYS`), and the **gap is checked before the cap**, because budget left is not permission to burst — that is the strategy's own "a real ceiling rather than a burst". The governor lowers a tier at under 25% over 30 days and **stops at 5**: five is the community agreement's own floor, so taking it would withdraw what the parent was promised in exchange for answering. `RESPONSE_MIN_SAMPLE = 4` is a judgement the estimate does not make — without it a contributor's very first unanswered request is a 0% rate and lowers their tier before they could answer a second. |

(2) *Shared, vouched and validated are three claims and exactly one is made.* Vouched needs a parent who would actually recommend it (`recommendation in ('yes','yes_with_caveats')`) — a parent can tell Pando about a class and stop short of recommending it, and without that distinction "vouched" means nothing. Two firsthand parents is *validated* regardless of enthusiasm: the label reports how many have used it. (3) *Freshness is recomputed from the record's own date, never read from `shares.freshness_state`.* Nothing walks that column nightly yet — that is the ping job (10.3) and it does not exist — so a stored `fresh` on a fourteen-month-old record would be the app asserting something untrue. Per category, from the seeded `freshness_policy`: 130 days is stale for a class and still fresh for a playground. An unknown category takes the **strictest** policy, and a missing date is `stale`, never `fresh`. (4) *A stale record is marked, never hidden* — the spec's answer to old knowledge is to label it, so `usable()` refuses only what no label can fix: an unreviewed record, and firsthand experience on a row nobody submitted as a parent. |

But three queues are not lists of short values, they are *records*: eight to ten attributes, several of them whole sentences a parent wrote, plus three or four actions each. Forced into a table, `/admin/activities` rendered its "Counts toward Founding" cell as **six lines of one word each** down a 90px column, beside an actions column where "Add to Pando" wrapped mid-phrase across three lines; `/admin/caregivers` did the same with four stacked buttons; and `/admin/demand`'s primary button read **"I've dealt with this…"** — an ellipsis eating the label of the button an admin is meant to press. **A table's contract is that a column means the same thing on every row and can be scanned down. Once a cell holds a sentence that contract is void**, and the column is just a narrow box with prose in it. So those three are `components/admin/Record.tsx` now — `RecordCard` with a header (title · kind · badges · who and when), a `FactGrid` that drops a column rather than squeezing one below a readable width, `Quote` for the parent's own words, and `RecordDrawer` for the panel an action opens. **`Quote` is not decoration**: invariant 8 turns on the difference between what a parent wrote and what the system says about it, and a reviewer working a queue has to see that difference without checking — so italic quoted prose against a green rule is reserved for it and the admin's own sentences never wear it. **`/admin/contributors` stays a table**, deliberately, and that is the rule rather than an exception: its values are all short. **Four rules worth keeping.** (1) *`Button` is `whitespace-nowrap`*, which is a correctness fix — a button is as wide as its words, and if that is too wide for its container then the container is wrong. (2) *`FactGrid` for a handful of mixed-length facts a reader scans across; `SpecList` for a long run of short values a reader scans down and compares between records* (`/admin/claims` had already written the second one privately, making it the fourth in-page implementation of "label, value"). (3) *A wash goes on the exception, never the rule* — every non-ordinary demand row used to carry one, so in the "needs a person" view the whole page was pink while two banners above it made the same point in words; only the allegation class keeps it, because that is the one class where Pando does nothing at all until a person has read it. (4) *An empty value renders an em dash*, so a gap reads as "they skipped this" and not as a layout bug — except where the absence is itself the point (a caregiver's consent evidence says "None recorded" out loud). |

"primary" : "secondary"}` wrapped in their own `useEdgeFade` copy, `/admin/delivery` and `/admin/responses` had the same trick with no scroll handling at all, and `/admin/contributors` had bespoke pill markup with its own colours. One control on five pages under three appearances, which is the fault `labels.ts` was written to end for *words*, in layout. `SegmentedFilter` is the one now, and it carries two rules the copies did not: **a filter is not a primary action** (reusing the green `primary` button for "this tab is selected" put the loudest thing on the page on a control that changes what you are *looking at*, sitting next to real primary buttons that change the data — selected is `green-wash` with a green rule), and **it says what it is** (`role="tablist"` with `aria-selected`, so a reader hears "3 of 6" instead of six unrelated buttons). It also carries per-option counts, which turned up a real defect on the way: `/admin/demand`'s "Needs a person" tab said **14** and listed **19**, because the label added `high + peer` while the tab filters on `requires_human_review` — so the five allegation questions, the class the page treats as most serious, were the five its own counter left out. The rule that prevents the next one: **a count and the list it describes come from one expression, never from two that happen to agree.** `Toolbar` is the other half, and it exists for one specific defect: four controls in `PageHead`'s `right` slot, which is a `flex` of `shrink-0` items, and the last of them was a checkbox whose label — the one thing in a row with no intrinsic width to defend — **wrapped into three lines of one word each** against the page title. **And the trap that cost the most to find:** appending `w-auto` to `inputClass` does nothing. Two utilities for the same property in the same layer are resolved by Tailwind's own output order, not by where they sit in the string (the lesson `Chip.tsx` already records for `active:scale-*`), so `` `${inputClass} w-auto` `` kept `w-full` and three toolbar controls meant to sit in a row stacked full-width down the page — with a class list that reads exactly as intended. Hence `controlClass` (the look, no width) with `inputClass` derived from it. Measured in the DOM afterwards, not assumed: 208 / 161 / 168px in a row. M5, M7, M9 and M10 are all built and **none of them had ever run end to end**, because the only way to see what Pando says was to have it text somebody. **What changed is the provider step and nothing above it.** `sendSms` keeps its whole order — opt-out → quiet hours → contributor protection → provider (invariant 6) — and `transportFor` picks the transport at step 4. That is the point rather than a convenience: a test channel that bypassed the gate would exercise a code path that will never ship, and a second send path beside `sendSms` is the thing invariant 6 exists to forbid. Walked live: an opted-out person is refused with **nothing reaching the channel at all**, and outreach with no `personId` is still refused. **Verification is pinned to the real provider, by an explicit marker.** `purpose: "verification"` on the one call in `/api/seed/verify/start`, because a code posted into a Slack channel is a code the parent never receives. **Routing on `category` would have been exactly backwards** — `transactional` covers HELP, the settings reply and every capture prompt, i.e. most of what the relay exists to exercise — so `test:relay` asserts both that the marker is there and that the router does *not* read the category. **One channel, and threads are the addressing scheme.** Every post names its recipient (masked — see below) and its Slack `ts` is stored in `message_log.provider_message_id`, so a reply *inside the thread* resolves back through `personForThread` to the phone number the pipeline works in. A top-level `+16265550143: hello` is the other door, and it exists for one reason: 5.9's cold inbound is otherwise untestable, because a stranger has no thread by definition. Anything else in the channel is ignored — a human talking to a human is not an inbound text. **The hole the walk found, which no amount of reading would have:** a keyword reply (HELP, the START confirmation) is sent *before* the pipeline ensures a person, so it carries no `personId`. On a phone that is invisible, because the number **is** the address; in one channel it posted "unknown recipient" outside every thread, and the parent's next reply had nothing to resolve against. `relayTargetFor` now resolves by phone when there is no id — the way SMS always did — and the relay writes the `message_log` row even when the caller could not name the person, because that row *is* the thread. It cannot inflate anybody's limits: every counter in `outreachAllowed` filters `category = 'outreach'` and these are `transactional` — checked in the source, not assumed. **Invariant 7 bends here in exactly one place, deliberately.** The invariant is about *logs*; this posts message bodies, which is the payload of the channel it stands in for. What it still refuses is a **full phone number** in the channel — the recipient is a masked number plus a first name, the pairing every admin surface uses. ⚠️ **So it must never be enabled against real contributors:** one channel holding every message Pando sends is a transcript of the network. It comes off on the same deadline as `SEED_VERIFY_DEV_CODES` and the `pando` starter password. **Two traps worth keeping.** `next start` "does not work with output: standalone" — and served requests anyway, from a bundle predating the module under test, which surfaced as a post arriving at the **real** slack.com with `invalid_auth`; the walk runs `.next/standalone/server.js`, the thing the build produces. And `spawn` with `shell: true` on Windows gives you the shell's pid, so `kill` left Next holding the port and the *next* run was answered by the orphan — every assertion after the signature checks failed, and the cause was only visible in the app's own stderr. `npm run test:relay` (21, pure) · `npm run test:relay-live` (25, a real server against a stubbed Slack). |
| **The eight admin actions that answered `400` are reachable, and the route argues about them properly** (2 Sep) | Flagged on 2 Sep and fixed here, because it is what blocks testing the very thing the Slack relay exists for: `answer.approve` · `answer.send` · `answer.reject` · `answer.edit` · `blast.send` · `blast_response.rate` · `blast_response.approve` · `blast_response.reject` were all implemented in `admin-write.ts`, wired to buttons on `/admin/answers` and `/admin/responses`, and **absent from `/api/admin/action`'s allowlist** — so M5's answer queue and M7's response review could not be worked at all. All 37 implemented actions are now reachable (asserted by diffing the switch against the set). **The validation is the part that needed thought, not the allowlist.** A rejection carries a reason, like `claim.decline` — the audit row is the only record of why a queue was cleared. An edited answer cannot be empty, because 5.8 sends `answer_text` **verbatim** on approval, so whatever passes there is exactly what a parent reads. A rating is refused outside 1–5 rather than clamped: `admin-write.ts` clamps with `Math.min(5, Math.max(1, …))`, which is right as a last line and wrong as the only one — an admin who typed 9 would get a stored 5 with nothing saying so, the same class as the matching weight Postgres would have rounded. And approving a blast reply must name a record **or** a merge target, because 7.9's whole point is that merging is offered beside creating (two parents on one record is "Validated by multiple parents"; two records with one parent each is nothing) — without either, `admin-write.ts` would default the kind to `activity` and create a record named after nothing. `SHARE_KINDS` is the enum's four members, not a guess: a camp is a first-class *taxonomy* category and has never been a share kind, the trap `lib/capture.ts` already paid for once. |
| **RCS needed the channel named, so the participation screen takes its own consent** (2 Sep) | The client's words: *"For RCS we need to add some opt in language."* Her paragraph goes above Continue on the participation screen, with a checkbox and Terms · Privacy linking to the site's own pages. **Why it is a second SMS-shaped consent rather than a reworded first one.** `sms` is taken at the phone field and authorises texting the number at all — it is what sends the verification code. This one is taken where the parent *chooses how often they may be asked*, and it covers what that choice implies: **recurring automated** messages, **SMS and RCS** by name, and a volume tied to the level just picked. Different words at a different moment, so `sms_recurring` is its own scope with its own version string (drizzle `0028` widens `consents_scope_check`) — a stored consent has to resolve to the text that was on screen, which is the one thing a TCPA complaint actually tests. **Five rules.** (1) *The checkbox gates the screen*, exactly like `/join`'s: an unchecked box that lets the parent through is not an opt-in, it is a decoration that would leave Pando messaging somebody who never agreed. (2) *The anonymous path is never shown it.* That route gave up SMS at `/join` in so many words and has no number stored, so there is nothing this could permit — and gating it would make the profile uncompletable for the one path that deliberately opted out. Verified: no block, no gate, dock open. (3) *There is no `declined` state.* Unticking writes `null` and re-locks the dock, so no row can ever say Pando asked and was refused — the route drops a `declined` even if one is posted, and `test:e2e` asserts that. (4) *The label covers the sentence, not the paragraph* — the same split as `SMS_CONSENT_AGREEMENT`, with the carrier disclosure beside it through `aria-describedby`, because a `<label>` wrapping a screenful of legal copy turns it into an opt-in control and an accidental opt-in is the worst failure this control has. (5) *"Terms · Privacy" is not in the stored string.* Those are links, not language, so they can never end up inside a consent record. **⚠️ One thing to put back to her:** her sentence says "tapping **Continue**", and on that screen the dock button reads **Review** — it is the last question before the review step. Registered copy and a flow control, so neither was changed unilaterally. Walked end to end against the live database: both rows land (`sms` + `sms_recurring`), each under its own version, and `npm run test:e2e` is **282** checks — asserting the landed row rather than the 200, because a route that accepted the field while the CHECK still refused the scope would have failed one layer down, which is exactly how the 18 Aug allowance change was caught. |
| **Two review labels, and one of them was worse than what she reported** (2 Sep) | Her note was *"MONTHLY ALLOWANCE → COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION"* and *"COST PREFERENCE → PRICE & VALUE"*. The second was exactly as she saw it. The first no longer existed: the 1 Sep rewrite had left that question's label reading **"Choose one"**, so the review row said "CHOOSE ONE · Community member" — an instruction where a summary label belongs, which says nothing about what the row is. So the rename fixes her request and a defect she could not have known about from the older build she was looking at. **Why editing `label` is safe here:** `ProfileFlow` renders a question's label above its chips only on a screen carrying **more than one** question (`questions.length > 1 ? question.label : undefined`), and both of these screens carry one — so the string is used only for the review row and the chip group's accessible name, and both improve. **And one left alone deliberately:** "Usual travel time — choose one" reads oddly on the review screen for the same reason, but that screen has two questions, so the label *is* on screen — and "Choose one" there is her own 24 Aug instruction (item 12: *"Travel time is single-select. Add 'Choose one' beneath 'Usual travel time'"*). Fixing the review row would mean a review-specific label, which is new API for one cosmetic row. |
| **A reassurance under a button that says "Continue where you left off" is answering a question the parent already moved past** (2 Sep) | Her instruction: remove the small "No app. No account. No password." tag from the landing screen's dock. It is a reassurance about *signing up*, and by the time that dock reads "Continue where you left off" the parent has already signed up and come back — so it sat in the one slot where the screen should be quiet, saying something they had answered for themselves. The dock's other line stays: when the button is **disabled** it says what is missing ("Add your name and number, and tick the box, to join"), which is the reason the button will not respond and belongs there. So the footer now says something only when it has something to say. **Not removed from the invite-code gate**, which has its own copy of the tag: that is a different screen (*"This link needs its code"*), she did not point at it, and a stranger who has just hit a gate is exactly the reader the reassurance was written for. |
| **A button and a link put their label in different places — so the box is a component's job now** (2 Sep) | The client's report was two controls on `/join` sitting at different heights ("одна кнопка вище, інша нижче"). The cause is a browser default nobody would think to check: **a `<button>` centres its own label vertically, a blockified `<a>` leaves its text at the top of the box.** Both were `min-h-11` in one `items-center` flex row, so both boxes were 44px and aligned — and the labels measured **11.5px apart**. `items-center` aligns boxes, which was never the problem, and the `self-center` somebody had added to the anchor was inert. **This is the eleven-copies fault, one layer down.** The quiet text action was hand-written **eleven times** across the seed, caregiver and chat surfaces, drifting on five axes — three font sizes (13.5 / 14 / 14.5), two tones, three disabled treatments, some underlined and some not, and **exactly one of the eleven** set a box model. `components/ui/TextAction.tsx` is the one now, and it renders `<button>` or `<a>`/`Link` **itself** rather than exporting a class string — because the call site is precisely where the two elements look interchangeable and are not. **Three decisions it settles.** (1) *One size, 14px* — the design system names that step "Help / secondary" and says not to invent sizes between the steps; 13.5 and 14.5 were exactly that, and five of the eleven were already at 14. (2) *`green` is an action, `quiet` is an aside* — green-deep for something the parent may well do next, muted-warming-to-green for the way out of the current path. (3) *A disabled text action loses its underline* rather than fading: `disabled:opacity-50` was one of the three treatments and it is the wrong one, because fading already-muted text drops it under AA **and** leaves the thing still looking tappable. **`InlineAction` is deliberately a second component, not a variant.** A link inside a sentence cannot be `inline-flex min-h-11` without blowing the line box apart, and **44px is not the target for it** — WCAG 2.5.8 exempts a target "in a sentence, or otherwise constrained by the line-height of non-target text". The rule is instead *as large as the line allows*: `-my-1 py-1`, which the consent line on `/join` had already discovered and which the resume banner and `WhatsNext` had not, so the two smallest targets in the parent flow (**23.6px**) were the two nobody had thought about. Now 31.6px. Measured honestly rather than claimed: the trick is *exactly* neutral on the consent paragraph and costs **2px** on the banner, because an inline-block's baseline alignment is not quite a text run's — a good trade for 8px of hit area, but a trade, so it is not for anything that must land on an exact grid. |
| **The parent flow had no card primitive, so `/join` changed elevation depending on which branch you were in** (2 Sep) | The admin has had `Card` since it was written; the seed flow never got one, so the same box was hand-written **fourteen times** and every site re-decided three things. **The visible cost:** on `/join` the founding card was `p-5 shadow-card` and the anonymous card that directly replaces it in the same slot was `p-5` with no shadow — tap "Share without joining for now" and the card under your thumb goes flat. Nothing intended that; it is two writings of one box. **The invisible cost:** a green-wash panel needs a `text-green-deep` heading and a gold one `text-gold-ink`, and all eight got it right *by memory*. `components/ui/Panel.tsx` derives the heading colour from `tone`, so the pairing cannot drift, and it settles the 1.1 / 1.15rem split the copies had (both sit inside the design system's "Card heading 1.1–1.15rem", which is how it went unnoticed). **Four rules.** (1) *`tone` is what the box means*, not how it looks: `card` neutral, `positive` for reassurance, `warning` gold — which in Pando means *pending or needs care* and never danger, since `alert` red is reserved for what is owed a person today and never appears in the parent flow at all. (2) *`raised` is separate from tone*, because the rule is elevation for **the one card the screen is about** and everything else earns its edge from a border — as a prop that stays a decision per screen rather than a property of a colour. (3) *`flush` for a panel whose children divide themselves* — the review screen's definition list, the chat recap's rows — which is also why `as` exists: `Panel as="dl"` keeps the review screen a real `<dl>` instead of trading semantics for a shared box. (4) *`className` is spacing only.* The box itself is not overridable, or the fourteen copies come back one `className` at a time. Walked at 375px afterwards: both `/join` branches now raise identically, the review `<dl>` is still a `DL` with clipped corners and 23 rows, the high-stakes D1 panel takes gold-wash with a gold-ink heading it never states, and no screen scrolls horizontally. |
| **The coefficients are the control now, and the prose explaining them is gone** (2 Sep) | The client's instruction on the harness, one day after the row below: *remove the descriptive text about the weights and the calculations, leave only the coefficients, and let me change them.* So `Explainer` comes off `/admin/matching` **in full** — including its first paragraph, which was about what the list is rather than about scoring — and the weight table it contained is now `WeightsCard`: the same numbers as fields, saved through a new audited action. **Read the row below before assuming this reverses it.** That pass answered *"the scoring logic is not entirely clear"* two ways at once — sentences on the page **and** the points on every badge — and only the sentences have gone. The arithmetic did not become unexplained, it became demonstrated: each row still shows the points each reason contributed, so the badges add up to the score beside them, and what went was the paragraph promising they would. **What this does change is the page's write surface, and that is the part a future session must not read as harmless.** The 27 Aug rule was *"there is no write action for this resource at all"*, and the reason was that one screen able to both score and message puts the pilot's first blast a mis-click from a page built for experimenting. That reason is untouched — nothing here sends anything, there is still no send button, and `matching.weight` writes **configuration**. But configuration is not nothing: weights are read from `affinity_weights` inside the scoring query on every run (§18.1 over §8.1), so a saved number changes who Pando asks on the next question with no deploy and no backfill — which is exactly why it is an audited admin action rather than a knob, and why the alternative it replaces was a hand-written UPDATE, the one kind of change to matching that leaves no audit row at all. **Six rules.** (1) *A conditional UPDATE, never an upsert* — `matching.ts` reads a weight as `config.weights[type] ?? 0`, so a row for a kind of connection the scorer never looks up would be a number on a screen that does nothing, and the admin who typed it would have no way to tell; zero rows updated answers `not_found`. (2) *Nothing is saved until Save, and only what changed is sent, one action per weight* — a field writing on every keystroke would put "5", "55" and "555" through three audited writes and three re-scorings on the way to a typo, and a single bulk action would record five decisions as one audit row. (3) *The route validates the number even though the database refuses it*, and the two refusals are different: `weight > 0` is a CHECK violation, while a fraction against an `integer` column comes back as `invalid input syntax for type integer` — **walked against the live database, because the plausible guess was that Postgres would round it** and hand the page back a number nobody chose. Both would reach the admin as a bare 502; the route turns them into a sentence. The 1–20 ceiling is arbitrary but not pointless: the seeded weights run 1–5, and a keystroke turning "5" into "55" would silently make one kind of connection outrank every other put together. (4) *The context step is shown and cannot be edited* — `RELEVANCE_STEP` is a constant in `lib/matching.ts`, not a row in the table, because the whole of life relevance being worth less than one shared school is a code decision; hiding it would leave the context points in every badge unaccountable, and rendering it as a field would claim a state nothing maintains. (5) *The list is ordered by name, not by weight* — heaviest-first reads well in a list and is wrong for a set of inputs, because a field jumps position the moment its value changes, under the cursor of whoever just typed in it. (6) *The weights come back with no parent chosen*, one extra query on a page that has not scored anything: configuration reachable only by first picking somebody to rank is configuration hidden behind an unrelated step. **And `adjacency_pairs` is gone from the payload** rather than left unrendered — a number sent to a page that no longer shows it is the dead-payload fault estimate 2.2 already cost us once. |
| **The descriptive box is off the profile pages, and on one of them it was a consent** (2 Sep) | Her instruction, with two screens shown: the boxed grey paragraph under the topic chips. Both are gone — `topics` and `topics_lived` no longer carry a `footnote`, so `ProfileFlow` renders nothing under the questions. **The part to raise rather than absorb quietly: on `topics_lived` that box was the listening-ear consent she herself dictated on 1 Sep**, when she removed the separate listening-ear page precisely because *"the Parenting Experiences page already acts as the topic-level opt-in"* and asked for those words to be added directly beneath the topics. Removing them does not remove the opt-in — selecting a topic is still what grants it, nothing is preselected, and `help` still tells the parent they decide whether to answer each time — but it is now granted by a tap and **stated nowhere the parent can read it**, and the four routing constraints it carried (topics only, never infer, urgent questions never routed as peer questions, names not shared) are code-only comments. That is a product decision about informed consent, so it is written here and put back to her rather than filed as a copy edit. **Two things deliberately left standing.** The privacy screen's caveat — *"members may sometimes be able to guess who you are, particularly in a small community"* — is the same kind of box on a screen she did not show, and it is the one thing that control cannot promise, so it needs her word before it goes. And the four directory footnotes (*"It doesn't have to be in your own city"*) are a one-line grey hint under a search box rather than the boxed panel she pointed at. **The test inverts rather than disappears**: `test:feedback` now asserts the box is *absent* from both screens and that the screen's own `help` line survives, so a future session cannot restore her 1 Sep footnote without her asking, nor strip the sentence the parent actually acts on. |
| **The picker is one control, and the score shows its own arithmetic** (2 Sep) | The second pass at the row below, and the reason it needed one is the lesson worth keeping: **both of her complaints came back, because both fixes were the cheapest shape of the right idea.** A search box was added — as a text input stacked on top of the `<select>` it filtered, so typing in one while the other sat unchanged beneath it read as a search that did not work. And a line was added above the table saying what the score was — while the numbers themselves were still explained only in a `title` attribute, which is the failure `labels.ts` exists to end one layer up: the words were written and nobody could reach them. **Three structural changes.** (1) *`components/admin/PersonPicker.tsx` is a real combobox* — `role="combobox"` with `aria-activedescendant` over a `role="listbox"`, arrow keys moving an active row without moving focus, Enter committing, **Escape reverting rather than clearing** (opening the list to look is not changing your mind about who you picked), and the matched text emphasised in each row so a reader can see *why* a row is there — "san" matching an *area* rather than a name is otherwise invisible, and a reader who cannot see why a row is present cannot trust the ones that are missing. Its ranking is pure and lives in `lib/admin/person-search.ts`, on the `lib/starters.ts` precedent: a matching rule inside a `useMemo` fails silently, and this one has the same failure mode as the area-slug bug — it looks fine right up until the one name somebody types is the one it cannot find. `npm run test:person-search` (24 checks). Two of its rules are load-bearing: **a slug is searched as words**, because `south-pasadena` has to answer "south pas" (the rule the inbound parser learned first), and **every term must match something** — an OR would answer "sarah south" with every Sarah *plus* everyone in South Pasadena, which is a longer list than the reader started with. The old select's special case for keeping the chosen row inside the filtered list is **gone rather than ported**: a combobox shows its selection in its own input, so nothing has to be pinned into a list of suggestions. (2) *Every reason badge carries the points it contributed*, plus the school or club where that is the interesting fact (`matchReasonValue`) — so "Same school: Field Elementary +5" and "Children the same stage +2" and "Similar practical constraints +0.5" visibly **add up to** the 7.5 beside them. That is the whole answer to "the scoring logic is not entirely clear": the row *is* the arithmetic, checkable rather than assertable. (3) *`Explainer` puts the sentences on the page* — a `<details open>` in `components/admin/ui.tsx`, with the weight table **inside** it instead of in a separate card at the foot of the page, where it explained badges a screen-height away. Open by default on purpose: a collapsed explainer is a tooltip with a bigger target, and the reader still has to know to look. **And one real bug, findable only in the DOM.** The score cell rendered `7.5` immediately followed by `7 connections` as **"7.57 connections"** — a wrong number on screen, not merely an unclear one. The total is its own block now, the breakdown its own line, neither able to wrap into the other. The same JSX whitespace trap `shortfall` documents had also bitten the flat-tail line ("The last 3rows score exactly the same"), so that sentence is built in JS too. `lib/matching.ts` is untouched and `npm run test:matching` (44) is unchanged — the scorer was never the problem, twice over. |
| **A caregiver can delete themselves by text now, and it is a real delete** (3 Sep) | 11.3, and the reason it waited is recorded rather than an oversight: *"the inbound `DELETE` keyword itself is Phase 2 (there is no channel yet)"* (12 Aug). The channel exists — Twilio's door and the Slack relay both feed one `handleInboundMessage` — so the sentence the 2C flow has been showing since 11 Aug ("Text DELETE and the whole profile goes, without asking why") is finally kept by the thing that says it. **The cascade is one copy, extracted.** `deleteCaregiverClaim` moved out of `claim.delete` into `repo/caregiver.ts` and both callers use it: two versions of a statement that removes a person's record would drift in exactly the place where drifting leaves a profile half-deleted. **Four rules.** (1) *It runs **before** `ensureInboundPerson`* — that function creates a nameless person for an unknown number (5.9), so running it first would mean answering a request to delete records by creating one; a stranger who texts DELETE leaves no trace of having done so. (2) *It sits with the keywords, not below them*, because DELETE is a decision **about Pando** rather than an answer to anything — the same reasoning that puts STOP first and keeps it away from the classifier. A caregiver mid-capture who texts DELETE means it. (3) *Exact on the whole message, and stricter here than anywhere else because this one is irreversible*: "delete my saturday slot" must not remove somebody's profile, and a substring test would. (4) *It acts immediately with no "are you sure?"* — "at any time by texting DELETE" describes one message, not two, and a confirmation step would make the copy false on the one screen where somebody is deciding whether to trust Pando. **Three outcomes, three true things to say**, and the third is the one worth having: an unreachable database reports `unavailable` rather than "you had no profile", because telling somebody who *does* have one that they never did is the single lie this feature exists not to tell. **What survives, deliberately:** `message_log.person_id` is `on delete set null`, so the row stays and the person is gone from it — the counts delivery monitoring reads stay true, and "the whole profile goes" was always about the profile rather than about the arithmetic of how many messages Pando has sent. The audit row carries **how they asked and nothing else** — `resource_id` is null on purpose, because storing the id of the profile just deleted would keep a pointer to the person the row exists to have removed. Walked live: a caregiver-only person goes entirely; somebody who is **both** a contributing parent and a signed-up caregiver keeps the parent half, including the `sms` consent that keeps the A2P defence file intact for that number. |
| **14.4 and 14.6 are what finally read what M9 and M10 wrote** (3 Sep) | Four things were being written and shown nowhere: `impact_events`, `freshness_pings`, `answers.helped`, and the tier `tierFor` computes. Correct, live, and invisible — which is worse than missing, because nothing looks wrong. **14.4 is a third view on `/admin/contributors`**, not its own nav item, on the 13 Aug precedent exactly: the consent file was moved there because *"as a separate nav item nobody could tell what it was for, and it is the same people asked a different question"*, and standing is the same people asked a third. It is the **first consumer `lib/tiers.ts` has ever had**. Three rules the numbers keep: the tier is recomputed every read and never stored (9.4 — a nightly write would only create the copy that goes stale, the `matching.ts` age-band argument); a response rate below `RESPONSE_MIN_SAMPLE` is **not shown at all** but said in words ("too few to judge"), because one miss out of one is a 0% rate and putting it on screen would show a contributor's first miss as a verdict; and the page states outright that the tier is earned access rather than a score, that nobody is shown their own, and that *being asked less* is a separate mechanism from *losing a rung* — the governor lowers the ask rate and never the tier. **14.6 is `/admin/impact`, and its shape is the point**: one row per answer joining 9.1's "did it help" to 9.2's "were they thanked", because the interesting case is invisible from either half — a **yes whose contributors were never thanked** is an ordinary answered prompt from one side and a plain absence from the other, and only together is it a loop that stopped. That join exists only because `drizzle/0026` added `share_ids`. "No reply" is its own tab and its own badge, never folded into the noes, because a silence is not a verdict (9.1) and a reader who read null as failure would draw exactly the wrong conclusion about a good record. **Neither page has an action, and 14.6's absence of one is deliberate**: a thank-you goes out through `thanks_delivery`, which batches one per contributor per week, so a button here would be a second sender with no knowledge of that week. Nor does it carry a nav count — the number worth acting on is "a yes still unthanked", which the weekly job is supposed to clear itself, so a non-zero badge would mean *the job is not running*, which is a different alarm and belongs in the container log rather than in a sidebar that cries wolf. **The bug found only by measuring the DOM**: the tier badge and the "1 more for Trusted" line reported the same `left`, i.e. the second had wrapped onto its own line while the markup said inline with an `ml-2` — the same shape as the matching page's "7.57 connections". It is a deliberate block now, and the wording went from "1 to Contributor", which read as a ratio, to "1 more for Contributor". Walked live: 6 Founding / 8 Contributor / 14 Member with Trusted and Local Expert both genuinely empty (nobody has three), a founding contributor showing no next rung because founding is not earned by volume, and an impact row reading `helped: true · contributors: 2 · unthanked: 2`. **Three fixture faults on the way, all of them the fixture**: `answers.labels` and `share_ids` are arrays and needed literals (the trap `repo/caregiver.ts` documents), `hold_reason` is NOT NULL because 5.8 requires every answer to carry why it waited, and the asker I picked had no phone. |
| **One address is often ten parents, and the first set of limits would have refused them** (3 Sep) | The most useful thing 15.4 turned up, and it came from `test:e2e` failing rather than from thinking harder. The first limits were sized for an attacker — five code sends per ten minutes per address — and the suite promptly failed with *"Too many code requests from here"*, because its walk needs **eleven code sends and twenty-four writes** from one address. **That is not a test problem. If a QA walk trips a limit, real people will.** And the real people here are specific: Pando's invite link is *"shared privately inside parent groups, not published"* (1.1), and parent groups **meet** — a mums' meetup, a school pickup, a preschool coffee morning. Ten parents behind one café or office NAT is one address to a per-IP limiter, so at five in ten minutes six of them would have locked each other out of signing up, at the exact moment the product is most likely to be adopted. So every number was raised to absorb a roomful (code sends 30/10min, writes 200/5min, search 600/min) and **the job of the per-address limit is only to stop a script** — a list-sprayer wants thousands a minute, not three. The protection that bounds a *targeted* attack was never this and is unchanged: **3 sends an hour per phone**, then a 15-minute lock on the number (§19, in the database, so clearing a cookie is not a way round it). Walked both ways against the running server afterwards: 30 sends allowed and the 31st refused, and the legitimate 282-check walk green. **The test's own assertion had to change shape too**, and that is the transferable part: it said "sending a code is the tightest limit in the app", which was true and was hostage to every other number — loosening the *search* ceiling for a roomful of parents would have failed a test about SMS billing. It now asserts the two orderings that actually have to hold (a send is tighter than a check, because a send is billed and a check is free; every write is tighter than the read) plus a floor that no limit may go below. |
| **`X-Forwarded-For` is read from the right, and the leftmost entry is a lie** (3 Sep) | 15.4's rate limiting, and the whole thing turns on one detail that is easy to get exactly backwards. A reverse proxy **appends** the address it saw to any `X-Forwarded-For` the request arrived with — so for a request that reached Traefik directly, the header this app sees is `<whatever the caller made up>, <the caller's real address>`, and the **leftmost** entry, which is the one every tutorial reaches for, is entirely under the caller's control. Trusting it gives a limiter that is bypassed by sending one header, which is **worse than no limiter**: it looks like protection and is not. So `clientAddress` counts from the right, one hop per trusted proxy, with `TRUSTED_PROXIES=1` because Traefik owns 80/443 on the VPS. Getting that number wrong in the *high* direction reads a caller-supplied value again, which is why it is a documented deployment variable rather than a constant. **Walked against the running server, not just asserted:** twenty requests through, then 429 with `Retry-After: 599`; then five more with a *different forged leading entry each time* — all still 429 — and three from a genuinely different last entry, all 200. **Four more rules.** (1) *A refused request does not increment the count*, so hammering a locked key cannot push the reset further out — a limiter that punishes retries turns an impatient double-tap into a five-minute wait, and is its own denial of service. (2) *No usable address means allow*, the one place this fails open, and deliberately: refusing every request from a deployment whose proxy headers are shaped unexpectedly takes the tool offline for people who did nothing wrong, which is the same reasoning as the invite-code fallback. It is logged, because a deployment where this is always null is misconfigured and nobody would otherwise find out. (3) *The limits differ by an order of magnitude because the costs do* — a code send is billed and can flag a 10DLC campaign, so it is the tightest and `test:security` asserts that it stays the tightest; a market search is a pooler round trip behind an invite-gated screen, so it has the highest ceiling and the aim is to stop a scrape starving real parents rather than to police browsing. (4) *The counters are in-process*, which is adequate for **one** container and stated rather than discovered: the day there are two, each enforces its own copy and the effective ceiling doubles. `consume` is pure and takes a bucket precisely so that moving the store to Postgres changes nothing else. Redis was not added — a new dependency, a new deploy and a new outage mode ("what does the app do when the limiter is down?") for a property this deployment does not have yet. |
| **The four webhooks are deliberately *not* rate-limited, and a test now proves nothing else is missed** (3 Sep) | The other half of 15.4, and the more valuable half. Four of the row's five items — database access rules, caregivers gated on consent, personal data not logged, all webhooks validated — are properties of the **whole codebase**, and a comment claiming them is true only on the day it is written. So `npm run test:security` walks `app/api` and fails on any route that is neither signature-verified, secret-gated, session-gated nor rate-limited. Nineteen routes, each printed with the guard that covers it. **Webhooks are exempt from limiting on purpose:** all four verify before anything reads the request and all four refuse when their secret is unset, and a limit there would throttle Twilio's and Stripe's own retries — which is how a delivery status or a payment gets lost. That is hardening in reverse. `/api/health` and `/api/seed/verify/status` are the two named exemptions, each with its reason in the set rather than in a commit message: one is what a monitor polls, the other is three booleans identical for every caller with no request object to key a limit on. **The audit found two things on its first run, and both are worth reading as a pattern.** Its first version listed the four signature functions by name and missed `checkSlackSignature`, reporting a signed route as unguarded — the way a structural test cries wolf and then gets ignored, so the pattern is now any `*Signature`. And invariant 7's check is a grep for the *shapes* that went wrong before (`phone:`, `body:`, `first_name:` as a logged field) rather than for the word "log", because CLAUDE.md records this repo already paying for it once: `withDb` falling back to a wrapper's `message`, which drizzle builds from the rendered SQL and every bind parameter. |
| **`caregivers_answerable` claimed to enforce invariant 1 and did not** (3 Sep) | The audit's real find, and it is a **dormant trap** rather than a live bug — which is the more interesting kind. The view was created in `drizzle/0001` filtering on `consent_status = 'consented' AND active`, while invariant 1 is four conditions; and the comment above it read *"matching, SMS answers and exports all read this — never `caregivers`"*, which is a promise that this is the safe way to reach a caregiver. Two conditions short, and `discoverable` is the one that matters: measured against the live cohort, **three** caregivers pass consented+active and only **two** also pass discoverable — so a caller that believed the comment would have surfaced somebody who agreed to be *listed* and declined to appear in *answers*. Consent is not visibility, and 2C makes that a real supported outcome. **Why it was dormant:** `grep` finds exactly one occurrence of the view in the repo — the `CREATE` itself. Nothing reads it. M5.5's `retrieval.ts` was written later and carries all four conditions inline, which is why `test:security` checks both. And `is_adult` could never have leaked either way, because it is `NOT NULL` with `CONSTRAINT adults_only CHECK (is_adult)` — invariant 2 enforced by the table, which no query can undo. **The fix is the view, not the comment** (`drizzle/0030`): a trap with a reassuring label is worse than no view, because the next person needing "the safe read" will find it and it will look right. `is_adult` is in the WHERE clause too even though it can never exclude a row — belt and braces against a future migration relaxing the CHECK. `introducible` is deliberately **not** a filter: invariant 1 excludes it in so many words, since being in an answer and being introduced are different amounts of exposure, so it stays a selected column a caller can read. Verified live: 3 pass the old two conditions, 2 come back through the view now. |
| **A limit of `max: 0` let one request through per window** (3 Sep) | Small, found by a test rather than by reading, and worth recording because of *how* it hid. `consume` treated a missing bucket as "fresh window, count 1, allow" — sensibly — and that branch never consulted `max`. So a limit of zero admitted **one request per window, forever**. Not reachable from `LIMITS` today, and fixed anyway: `max: 0` is the obvious way to close an endpoint in a hurry — a scrape in progress, a route being abused — and a switch labelled "off" that is really "one at a time" is exactly the quiet wrongness nobody would think to check for under pressure. The check now runs before every other branch. |
| **The estimate's price list is not the price list, and Stripe is told the amount rather than asked for it** (3 Sep) | Estimate 13.5 asks for "payment links using the configured tier prices (**$5 / $12 / $20 / $35**)". Those four numbers are **identical in the 26 Aug and the 3 Sep workbooks**, which is what settles it: they are not a new decision, they are the estimate's own pre-8.18 five-tier scheme. The client's *Pando Strategy — Current Direction* (8.18) §8 names four tiers and different money — **Passive free · Board Ask $5 · Targeted Ask $15 · Last-Minute Care free in the pilot** — and CLAUDE.md's rule is that the newer client document wins, already applied to these very tiers in `blast-tiers.ts` and `drizzle/0021`. So the consequence to state plainly: **$12, $20 and $35 are charged by nothing in this codebase**, and if she wants them the change is one edit in `blast-tiers.ts`. `npm run test:payments` asserts that only 500 and 1500 are chargeable anywhere, so a future session cannot "fix" the prices back without meeting it. **And there is no Stripe Price object.** `createCheckout` sends `price_data` inline, because a Price in the dashboard would be a second copy of a number this codebase owns, editable by somebody who cannot see the code, and it would disagree silently with the amount a refund reads back. What *is* stored is what was actually charged, frozen onto the blast row (`price_cents`) — the same reasoning `pool_target` already carries: a tier's configuration can change, and this parent was charged what was in force when they asked. Reading `TIERS[tier].price_cents` at refund time two weeks later would refund today's price, which is either short-changing somebody or paying them extra, and neither is recoverable from the row. **Two smaller rules.** The receipt line is its own field (`receipt_description`) rather than the admin-facing `note`, which opens with the price and carries asides like "The first one is free" — bewildering on something a parent just paid for; and **neither ever contains the question**, because a Stripe receipt is emailed, stored by a third party and often forwarded onward.
