And I wrote `sc.contributor_id`, which does not exist; the column is `person_id`, and the only reason it surfaced was reading the dev-server log rather than trusting a null result. |
| **Personal invitations are a group, not a per-parent link** (27 Aug) | The client's decision, and it **closes** the question that had been open since 31 July and reopened on 12 August: no unique link per founding contributor. A personal invitation is instead one more invite row whose group is "personal inviting" — so the existing shape carries it with **no code at all**: `invite.create` requires only `code` and `label`, `group_option_value` is optional, and two invites already run without one. **What this settles, and it is the reason the question kept coming back:** the three consequences named in the 12 Aug row stay in force as intended behaviour rather than as gaps — cross-device resume is impossible (autosave is device-local), D2 referral attribution stays an admin judgement rather than a URL, and the address stays `/join?i=…` rather than `/seed/[token]`. They were always a package; declining the package keeps all three. **What is still answerable:** "how many arrived by personal invitation, and how many of them contributed" — `people.invite_id` records the arrival, `invites.opens` the denominator, and since 27 Aug PostHog carries `invite_code` on every event, so that link segments its own funnel like any other. What it cannot answer is *which person* did the inviting; that remains the founding queue's own judgement, unchanged. |
| **One shared invite link**, not per-parent links (31 Jul · reaffirmed 12 Aug · settled 27 Aug) | Client's call. QC Answers Q3 asks for **both** — a unique link per founding contributor, pre-filling identity and enabling pre-linked auth — and that was put to QuitCode on 12 Aug and declined in favour of the original call. (The *group* half of what Q3 wanted is now covered by the row above, without per-parent links.) **Three consequences are therefore intended behaviour, not gaps:** returning from a second device is impossible (autosave is device-local, so Q4's "jump straight to *add another*" only holds on the same phone), D2 referral attribution stays an admin judgement rather than a URL, and the address stays `/join?i=…` rather than Q2's `/seed/[token]`. If it ever flips, all three flip together. |
| **"Network Ask"**, never "Network Check" (31 Jul) | Client renamed it. New promise attached: *no useful answer → not charged*. |
| **Founding is never self-granted** | There is an admin approval queue ("is this really Sarah from our group?"). It activates on the **second approved contribution**; 1.7 says "in review" and promises a text. |
| **Three separate consents**: follow-up ≠ Blast ≠ reference | Different levels of exposure. `lib/consent.ts` keeps the wording versioned. |
| **Reference willingness comes from the nominating parent**, not the caregiver | It is the parent who would be the reference. The caregiver's own consent covers being *listed*, and everything beyond it is a separate step in their own flow (2C) — Pando never contacts them to ask. |
| **Marketing at `/`, Seed Tool at `/join`** | `/?i=<code>` redirects, so links already shared keep working. |
| **Founding CTA points to `/join`**, not the Tally form | Client asked; the static HTML still shows Tally — ignore it. |
| **A modal never renders inside an animated wrapper — it portals to `body`** (5 Aug) | `animate-step-in` ends at `transform: none`, but a *filled* animation computes to the identity matrix, and any transform other than `none` makes that element the containing block for `position: fixed`. So `OtherSheet`'s "full-screen" overlay was clipped to the question block for the life of the screen — invisible on a phone, a half-width sheet anchored to the middle of the page on a laptop. It is the only `fixed` element in the app; the next one must portal too. |
| **No phone-frame on desktop** | We tried it; a 27rem ribbon with its own scrollbar was worse than either alternative. `md:` = real desktop layout. |
| **The measure is a ladder, not one number** (6 Aug) | `Container` was capped at `md:max-w-[40rem]` and stopped there, so on a 1920px window the app used 68% of it and stranded ~300px of empty paper either side. It now steps `40 → 46 → 52 → 62rem` across `md/xl/2xl/3xl`, and `BrandPanel` grows with it (`21 → 25 → 29 → 34rem`), measured back to 80% at 1920. Two things a future session should not "simplify": `--breakpoint-3xl` exists because Tailwind stops at 1536px while monitors don't, and chat bubbles are capped separately (`md:max-w-[34rem]`) because a percentage measure on a wide column turns one sentence into one line. **Beyond ~1920 the margins grow again on purpose** — the alternative is capping and centring the whole composition, which is the phone-frame idea above under a different name. |
| **The PostHog key is a build arg, not a server env value** (7 Aug) | Everything in `/docker/pando/.env` is read at request time — `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` is the exception, inlined into the client bundle by `next build`. A key set on the server does nothing, silently, and the failure looks like "analytics is broken" rather than "misconfigured". So `web/Dockerfile` takes `ARG NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY` before `RUN npm run build` and `.github/workflows/deploy.yml` passes it from a repo secret. Verified both ways: with the var set at build time the value appears in `.next/static/chunks/*.js`; without it the bundle is clean and the build still succeeds (analytics off, image fine). Changing the key means a redeploy, not a `docker compose up -d`. |
| **PostHog autocapture stays off; screen recording is off by default and switched on only for QA** (7 Aug · amended 19 Aug) | The 7 Aug half is unchanged and not negotiable: **autocapture** would violate invariant 7 on the first page load — it ships DOM attributes and input values off-app — so `lib/posthog-provider.tsx` sets `autocapture: false`, and every event Pando wants is already a named `track()` call in `lib/analytics.ts` with structured props, none of which carry free text. **What changed is session recording, and only its default.** `NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_SESSION_RECORDING=1` turns it on; unset — including a build that forgets the arg — leaves it off. Why it exists: the client walks this flow herself before any real parent does, and *“where did she get stuck”* is not answerable from named events, which say a screen was reached and never why it was hard. **Two things to understand before touching the switch.** (1) *There is no partial version of it.* The obvious safety valve — start recording only after the sensitive screens — is inverted here: rrweb takes a **full DOM snapshot when recording starts**, and `ProfileFlow`'s `review` stage, the chat transcript and `/done`'s “What you shared” each render **every earlier answer at once**, so the late screens are the densest, not the safest. Verified in a real browser, not assumed. (2) *The PostHog project's own settings cannot be the boundary.* `sessionRecording` is fully enabled in that project's remote config (`endpoint: "/s/"`, `maskAllInputs: true`) and was doing nothing only because the client-side flag overrode it — so the switch that matters is this one, in the repo, under review. `maskAllInputs` is close to worthless here anyway: Pando is tap-first, so a school name is a rendered chip, not an input value. **It comes out on the same deadline as `SEED_VERIFY_DEV_CODES` and the `pando` starter password: before the first real founding contributor.** Turning it off is a redeploy, not a server restart — it is a `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` build arg (`vars.NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_SESSION_RECORDING`), like the key above. |
| **A stored session is untrusted input** (4 Aug) | It was written by whatever build the parent last opened, and mid-pilot they meet several. `{...EMPTY_ANSWERS, ...stored}` looked safe but let a stored `null` overwrite a default `[]` — that crashed the profile screen. `normaliseAnswers` in `lib/storage.ts` now keeps a value only when its shape still matches, `selectionsFor` never returns null, and a chat draft pointing at a step that no longer exists is dropped back to the menu. |
| **Mobile is the design target** | Every desktop rule sits behind `md:`/`lg:`. Never regress the phone to improve the laptop. |
| Weights resolved from **config at query time**, not from the stored row | §8.1 vs §18.1 conflict; config wins or a weight change needs a backfill. |
| **The matching graph is derived on the server, from the answers — never taken from the request body** (11 Aug) | `lib/derive.ts` ran on the client and `/api/seed/profile` stored what it sent. That made the client the author of its own edges: which schools, groups and neighborhoods a parent is connected to, and therefore whose questions reach them and whose answers they see. CLAUDE.md calls that graph the long-term asset, so it was the wrong shape twice — a stale build writes stale weights, and a crafted request can assert an affinity with a school the parent has nothing to do with. **Verified by sending fabricated rows and confirming none survived**, while the real ones derived correctly with weights from the question set. The fix was cheap because `derive.ts` is pure with no browser dependency: the route calls *the same functions*, over answers it has already sanitised, and `derivePendingOptions` takes the market from the validated invite rather than the body. The client still sends its copy and is simply ignored — the payload contract only got narrower, so no build breaks. `raw_answers` remains the record of what was actually tapped, and re-deriving from it stays possible. |
| **Ingress on the VPS is Traefik, and `/docker/pando` is load-bearing** (5 Aug) | The box already runs Traefik (owning 80/443 + Let's Encrypt) and n8n; host `nginx` is inactive with a stock config. So no nginx/certbot — routing is container labels in `deploy/docker-compose.yml`, copied from the static site it replaced with the upstream port changed 80 → 3000. The compose project name comes from the directory, and that is what keeps the container on the `pando_default` network Traefik already reaches — moving or renaming the directory takes the site off Traefik and the only symptom is a 404 in front of a healthy app. |
| Deployment is **standalone Docker on our VPS**, not Vercel | See DEPLOY.md. Env is read at request time; `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` is not. |
| **A workflow's `persisted: false` is honoured, not overridden** (31 Jul) | Derive-only scenarios exist on purpose. The route must never report a save the backend didn't make. |
| **The backend is the Next.js app; Supabase is only the database** (6 Aug) | Reverses "business logic lives on the n8n canvas" (3 Aug), "two transports" (4 Aug), "Supabase never calls out" and "workflows built by the client". Client's call. Logic is TypeScript in `lib/server/repo/*` behind `lib/server/db.ts`; Drizzle owns the schema (`web/drizzle/`). **What we gave up:** Janet can no longer change a rule on a canvas — every change is now code plus a deploy, through us. **What we gained:** types, code review, and real transactions — the atomic "nomination + its restricted notes" and "every admin write carries its audit row" are now enforced by `BEGIN/COMMIT` rather than by node ordering. ~1000 lines of workflow JSON and the whole RPC layer (0003/0005/0006/0007) were deleted, not archived: a stale spec read as truth by the next session is worse than no spec. |
| **Safety invariants live in the route *and* in DB constraints** (31 Jul, still true) | Originally "not in an n8n Code node", because a Code node is edited in a UI without review. The reason survives the move: application code can be wrong too, so `consent_status`, `active`, the 18+ gate and initials-only stay `CHECK`s. `web/drizzle/0002_rls.sql` asserts eight of them at migration time — the only automated test in the repo. |
| **A saved card can be corrected field-by-field** (31 Jul) | Tapping a recap row re-asks that one step, seeded with the current answer, and re-saves under the same `client_id` (so the backend upserts). Adding a *skipped* field afterwards is still not possible. |
| **No query parameter changes app behaviour** (4 Aug, reversed `?test=1`) | `?i=` and `?src=` are the only ones read, and they are the product's own link. `?test=1` and a short-lived `?reset=1` are gone: a URL that quietly changes what gets stored is a footgun for whoever forwards the link, and the client wants to walk the flow as a parent would. `is_test` stays a column — the schema, payloads and every admin count still filter on it, so a row can be marked from the admin side or by a seed script. **Consequence to accept:** our own walkthroughs now look like real contributors, so clear them from the database rather than relying on a flag. |
| **The anonymous path never sees a Founding claim** (4 Aug) | The entry screen tells them in so many words that they give it up. The completion screen showed the badge anyway, which was the app contradicting its own promise on the last screen they see. `wants_founding === false` now drives the badge and the "what happens next" copy. |
| **Admin auth is per person, hashed — the actor is proved, not picked** (10 Aug) | Supersedes "one shared password + pick who you are" (31 Jul), which is what the client asked for and which had one flaw that outweighed its convenience: **every `audit_log` row is written from `session.user`, and `session.user` was self-asserted.** Anyone holding the shared password could sign in as Janet, so "the audit trail names a person" did not mean that person proved anything — on the one surface where a decision about a named caregiver has to carry the name of whoever made it. Now one scrypt record per person (`scrypt:N:r:p:salt:hash`) — **in `admin_users`** since 12 Aug, see the row below; no password is stored anywhere, and **the cost parameters live inside each record** so raising them later needs no migration and cannot silently mismatch. `ADMIN_CREDENTIALS` (the same records in an env var, `npm run admin:credential`) and then `ADMIN_PASSWORD` + `ADMIN_USERS` remain as fallbacks in that order — deprecated, announced on the sign-in screen — for one reason only: the deploy that adds credentials must not take the admin dark before the client has generated them. **Two things worth knowing:** an unknown name is hashed against a throwaway salt so response time cannot enumerate who has access (measured: ratio 1.00), and `ADMIN_SESSION_SECRET` should be set in production — with it, rotating one password ends one person's sessions; without it the signing key is derived from the records, so rotating anyone's signs everyone out. Both behaviours are asserted in `npm run test:auth` (45 checks) rather than assumed — an earlier version of that comment claimed the per-person behaviour held in both configurations, and the test proved it did not. |
| **The credential store is a table, and a populated one is authoritative** (12 Aug) | `ADMIN_CREDENTIALS` got the hashing right and the operations wrong: adding a person, rotating a password and removing someone who left were all an env edit plus a redeploy. **Revoking access needed a build**, which is the one operation that must never wait for one. `admin_users` (drizzle `0008`) holds the same scrypt record per person plus the three facts a string could not carry — `active`, `password_changed_at`, `last_sign_in_at` — and `npm run admin:user -- add\|list\|password\|disable\|enable` is the whole interface, each command writing its own `audit_log` row. **Four rules, none of them optional.** (1) *Authoritative:* if the table has anybody in it, the env vars are not read — otherwise a person revoked here walks back in through an `ADMIN_CREDENTIALS` entry nobody remembered to delete. (2) *Bootstrap:* an **empty** table falls through to the env, so the deploy that creates it does not take the admin dark. (3) *Fail closed:* if the store is configured and unreadable, the mode is `unavailable` — no sign-in, no session honoured, and the screen says so; falling back to the env during an outage is exactly how a revoked admin gets their access back, and it would buy nothing, because every admin page reads through the same database. (4) *Cached 60s* (`lib/server/admin-auth.ts`), because `proxy.ts` runs before every admin request and a round trip against the pooler is ~200ms warm, 1.3s cold — so a revocation lands within a minute, and immediately on the next sign-in, which always re-reads. **Deliberately not built:** a page for managing *other* admins. A session that can grant a session is a different security surface, the client asked for one admin and maybe two (QC Answers Q6), and a CLI leaves the change where an operator made it. |
| **The pilot starts on a shared starter password, and that is a dated decision** (12 Aug) | `janet` and `andrii` exist in `admin_users` with the password `pando` — hashed like any other (`scrypt:65536:8:1:…`), never stored or logged in the clear. It took `--insecure-password` to get past the CLI's 12-character floor, and the choice is written into the audit row as `weak_starter_password: true`, so "is the starter password still in place" is answerable months later without asking anyone. **The floor did not move:** the default `add` still refuses a short password, and `/api/admin/password` still requires 12 — the product's own path only goes upward, so the first time either of them changes it in `/admin/account` the weak one is gone for good. **What it costs while it stands:** five guessable characters open every parent's profile, every restricted note about a named caregiver, and the consent file with unmasked phone numbers. Fine for a laptop and sample data; **not fine on the day the first real founding contributor is stored** — the pilot must not open with this in place. |
| **An admin changes their own password in the app; everything else about access stays in the CLI** (12 Aug) | `/admin/account` + `POST /api/admin/password`. The line is drawn at *whose* row is written: this one can only ever touch the person already signed in, because the name comes from the verified token and is the only thing in the `where` clause. **Four rules it enforces, none of them optional.** (1) *The session is not enough* — the current password is required, or a live tab on a borrowed laptop could lock the owner out of their own account; that check is throttled on the same counter as sign-in, keyed to the person rather than the IP. (2) *It cannot create or revive anybody* — no insert, and `and active`, so a disabled admin with a stale tab cannot set a fresh password and walk back in. (3) *It re-issues the cookie* — rotating a hash changes the session key material, so without handing back a new token a successful change would look exactly like being logged out at random. (4) *It is its own route, never an `/api/admin/action`* — that endpoint builds its audit row from the request body, so a password sent through it would land in `audit_log.after` in plain text. The audit row here carries the actor, the action and the time, and nothing else. Asserted in `test:e2e`, including that the new password appears nowhere in the audit log. |
| **All admin data goes through two endpoints** (31 Jul) | `/api/admin/query` and `/api/admin/action` → `lib/server/repo/admin-read.ts` / `admin-write.ts`. One write path means one place that cannot forget the audit row — and since 6 Aug that row is written *in the same transaction* as the change, so it cannot be lost separately. Contract: `web/lib/admin/types.ts`, unchanged by the move. |
| **Against the pooler, round trips are the only cost that matters — so read paths minimise queries, not query complexity** (10 Aug) | Measured, because the admin felt slow and the obvious suspects were all wrong. Indexes were already on every join column; the heaviest admin query added **6ms** to a **~200–250ms** round trip. What actually cost time: (a) opening a connection is **~1300ms** of TLS + auth, and `idle_timeout: 20` threw the pool away between page loads — now 300s; (b) `contributorDetail` ran **11 queries in a `Promise.all`**, which reads like the fast version and is not — on a cold pool it opens eleven sockets and pays the handshake on ten of them. Collapsed to **one statement with scalar `json_agg` sub-selects: 1724ms → 485ms warm, 2491ms → 1664ms cold.** Also: `overview` scanned `founding_checklist` four times (the view runs two correlated counts *per person*) → one materialised CTE with `FILTER`; `contributors` asked that view for one column and then the other → one `LEFT JOIN`. **Two things a future session should not "fix":** fanning these back out into `Promise.all` makes it slower, not faster, and adding an index will not help a read that is already an index lookup — measure `select 1` first, and compare against it. One shape changed as a consequence: timestamps inside `json_build_object` are serialised by Postgres (`…680876+00:00`) rather than by the driver (`…680Z`); both parse to the same instant, but nothing may string-compare them.<br><br>**The floor is not `select 1`, it is `select 1` plus a parameter.** `prepare: false` costs an extra round trip for *any* parameterised statement — measured at 228ms with no parameters against ~410–490ms with one, and the same for two, for a uuid, a text, a bool or an int. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` on the detail query reports **0.3ms of execution and 0.5ms of planning**, so none of this is database work. `prepare: true` removes it (255ms with a parameter), and a stress test — 3600 statements over 30 sockets plus a re-run after an idle — raised no prepared-statement errors; but Supabase's own FAQ still says transaction mode does not support them while their changelog says named statements are now broadcast across connections, so **this stays `false` until the contradiction is settled.** `fetch_types: false` was also measured (saves ~270ms of the ~1300ms handshake) and **rejected**: it returns `text[]` columns as strings, which would silently break `topic_preferences`, `strengths`, `hold_reasons` and every other array in the app. |
| **The seed reward is one contribution; Founding is two** (10 Aug) | Two thresholds that look alike and are not. The kickoff call set the payment minimum at *"one activity or one caregiver"*; Founding activates on the **second** approved contribution. `reward_status` on `ContributorRow` is its own field for that reason — folding it into `qualifying_approved >= 2` would silently move whom the client owes money to. Both read the same `founding_checklist` view, so the definition of "qualifying" is written down once. **`none` is a deliberate state**, not an absence: the call asked to be able to see the parents who arrived and left nothing, because those are the ones who do *not* get paid. |
| **The contest has no threshold, so the app invents none** (10 Aug) | The client said more information earns entry into a $500 draw but never said how much more. The contributors list therefore sorts by approved contributions and stops there — a hard-coded "3+ enters the draw" would be a rule nobody agreed to, enforced by us. Open question in [docs/spec-compliance-review.md](docs/spec-compliance-review.md). |
| **A referral is recorded by an admin, never read from a URL** (10 Aug) | With **one shared invite link** there is no per-parent code to attribute, `invited_via_group` names a group rather than a person, and `?r=…` would breach "no query parameter changes app behaviour" (4 Aug). So `referral.link` is a human judgement — the same one the founding queue already makes — and the `referrals` row is its record. `profile_complete`, never `credited`: a credit is denominated in Network Asks, which do not exist until Phase 2, so granting one now would promise a balance nothing can spend. **If the client flips to unique links (v3.2 asks for them), this becomes automatic and the shape does not change.** |
| **A caregiver's own sign-up is a claim, not a listing** (11 Aug) | 2C had three constraints that ruled out the obvious design. The invite is `pando.is/caregiver` with **no token** — there is nothing to key one against, because Pando holds no contact detail for a nominee (invariant 13) — so a caregiver arrives self-identified and unlinked. Matching them to a nomination on a first name and an initial is the one thing `cards.ts` already refuses to do (two people called Maria G. are two people). And a self-made `caregivers` row would be indistinguishable from a parent's nomination, which is the hole invariant 4 exists to close. So `/caregiver` writes a **`caregiver_claims`** row keyed to their own verified `people` identity, invisible to every answering path, and an admin attaches it at `/admin/claims`. **Linking is the only place a caregiver reaches `consented`**, and it takes both halves: their yes (the claim cannot exist without it) and an admin's judgement that this claim is that person. It deliberately does **not** raise `active` / `discoverable` / `introducible` — consent is not visibility, and the ladder still only ever increases. `provenance` stays `parent_submitted`, so "vouched by a parent" keeps meaning what it says. |
| **The caregiver's three permissions are separate, and the larger cannot outlive the smaller** (11 Aug) | G9 (appear in an answer) and G10 (be introduced) are different amounts of exposure, so they are two questions and two consent records — and revoking G9 clears G10 in the UI, in the write route, *and* in a `claim_ladder_order` CHECK. Finishing the flow with all three refused is a real supported outcome: a profile that exists and is visible to nobody. The copy promises exactly that, so it has to be reachable. |
| **Recording caregiver consent requires evidence** (31 Jul) | Method is mandatory; a call or in-person yes also needs a note, because that note is the only artefact. Enforced in the route, not just the form. |
| **Pando never contacts a nominated caregiver** (3 Aug, July question set) | Reverses the earlier "admin-only number, deleted if they decline" design. We collect no contact details at all — the nominating parent sends an invite we generate (`lib/caregiver-invite.ts`), and nothing about the caregiver is stored until they set up their own profile. `contact` / `caregiver_phone` are refused by the save route even if an older client sends them. |
| **The subject table is `shares`, not `places`** (12 Aug) | `places` held four kinds of thing (`share_kind`): an activity, a camp, a place and a tip. A music class is not a place and a piece of advice certainly is not — so the name had been wrong since 0000 while the *structure* was right: one subject, many parents' contributions about it. Drizzle **0009** renames `places → shares`, `place_contributions → share_contributions`, `place_id → share_id`, and with them every constraint, index, trigger and the `shares_answerable` view — a `shares` table whose errors still said `places` would be the same lie one layer down. The admin action is now `share.answer_ready`, the row field `ContributionRow.share`, the flag subjects `share` / `share_contribution` (migrated with an UPDATE, since a flag names its subject by string rather than by key). **Do not confuse `shares` with `submissions`:** a submission is the card exactly as it was typed, kept to answer "did the parent actually say that"; a share is the thing it was about. Renames only — no data moved, and Postgres carried the views and foreign keys across by identity. |
| **`confidence` belongs to the contribution, not the place** (7 Aug) | Found the first time a real database was attached: `schema.ts` declared `confidence` on `shares`, while `flags.ts` (write + sweep) and `admin-read.ts` (the low-confidence count) all used `share_contributions.confidence`. The column simply did not exist, so **the whole admin overview query failed** and every extraction write was swallowed by `withDb`'s catch. Fixed additively in `drizzle/0003_add_contribution_confidence.sql`. The score describes one parent's free text (`what_makes_it_great`, `caveat`, `tip_text`, `who_for`, `who_not_for` — all columns of the contribution), not the venue, so the contribution is the right owner. **`shares.confidence` is now vestigial and read by nothing** — left in place rather than dropped, but if anyone "removes the duplicate", that is the one to remove. |
| **A database error is logged by its driver message only — never by a wrapper's** (7 Aug) | `withDb` read `err.cause` once and fell back to `err.message`. That fallback fired on the first real check-constraint violation and printed `DrizzleQueryError.message`, which drizzle builds from the rendered SQL **and every bind parameter** — so one failed profile write put a parent's phone number, both names, their neighborhood, their child's age and the whole `raw_answers` blob on stdout. Invariant 7, breached by the error path rather than by any log call. `driverError()` now walks to the innermost cause and identifies a wrapper by its `query`/`params` fields (postgres.js's `PostgresError` has neither), and there is **deliberately no fallback to a wrapper's message**: an unidentifiable error contributes its class name and nothing else. A lost diagnostic is recoverable; a logged phone number is not. Any future "simplification" back to `err.message ?? …` reintroduces the leak. |
| **`confidence` measures the text, and naming a person is not a defect in it** (12 Aug) | Found by probing the model on four contrasting cards rather than by reading the code, which looked fine. The schema said the score was for text that is "specific, concrete, **and about the place rather than about a person**" — so a card reading *"Ms. Diane got my son playing in three weeks after a year of refusing"* scored **0.35** and dropped into the low-confidence queue, while `possible_named_person` flagged it as well. It was penalised twice for the same thing, and it was the most useful card of the four. The same probe showed the second half of the fault: the model's reasons were "lacks cost", "lacks age ranges" — both **captured as taps** it could not see, so complete cards were marked down for columns that were full. Now the score answers one question — *how much could another parent act on this text* — the prompt says in so many words that naming a person does not lower it, and the taps are passed as context labelled "already answered". Same four cards after: 0.85 / 0.05 / **0.85 + flagged** / 0.85. Two `test:e2e` checks hold the line: the named-teacher card **must** be flagged and **must not** be under 0.6. **Do not re-merge the two signals to "simplify the schema"** — review and quality are different questions, and one queue exists for each. |
| **The extraction pass classifies; it never rewrites** (6 Aug) | 1.8 could have been built to clean up a parent's sentence for display. It isn't: invariant 8 says free text about a named person needs a human first, so the model returns a score, a boolean, and a reason — never text to publish. Two consequences that must hold: caregiver cards are excluded from extraction entirely (their free text *is* the restricted note, invariant 12), and a failed or declined extraction leaves `confidence` null rather than guessing — a wrong score would sort a card out of the low-confidence queue that exists to catch it. |
| **The closing question is routed, never just banked** (3 Aug) | D1 classifies into ordinary / peer-support / high-stakes, and since 11 Aug a fourth class below (`lib/demand.ts`). Peer support gets an in-flow answer and is stored only on an explicit yes; health-legal-safety gets professional resources immediately plus an admin flag. Category taps drive it; a keyword scan can only *escalate*, never de-escalate. |
| **The completion screen is three screens** (6 Aug) | Client's call, and a deliberate deviation from spec 1.7, which describes one screen. The single page carried the badge, the shared list, D1, the consent legal text, the OTP gate, five next-steps, the referral card *and* a come-back note — measured at ~3 000 characters, which nobody reads. Now `/done` tells (514 chars), `/done/ask` asks (1 117), `/done/next` explains (1 415). **The order is not free:** `submit()` sends `demand: session.demand` in the same completion write as the consent, so D1 must stay on the same screen as the follow-up buttons or ahead of them — putting it on `/done/next` would silently drop every demand signal. **The cost to accept:** two extra places to abandon before the one consent that matters, which is why `/done` names what's left on its own button and `seed_done_continue` / `seed_done_next_opened` exist to measure the drop-off. |
| **Founding activates on the 2nd approved contribution** (3 Aug) | Not on submission, and not on finishing the form. The completion screen says "in review" and promises a text when it activates. Secondhand contributions are welcome but never qualifying. |
| **Nothing is stored until the phone is verified** (3 Aug · the code sits at the end of the profile, 13 Aug) | Client's rule, verbatim: "if they abandon at OTP, nothing persists", enforced in `lib/server/gate.ts` and not only in the UI. **What changed is where the code is asked for, not the rule.** It sits at the **end of the profile** — after the questions, before anything is sent. It was moved to the entry screen on 12 Aug and moved off it again on 13 Aug: asking a parent to prove a phone number before they have seen a single question is the friction the client asked us to keep off the front door, and it read as "verification is broken" because a slow `/verify/status` left the gate undecided and waved them straight past it. The status is now **awaited** at the moment it is needed, so the step cannot be skipped by a race; if it cannot be fetched at all, the session falls back to holding rather than to guessing. Same guarantee — abandon at the code and nothing exists anywhere — but everything *after* it is stored as it is finished, so "saved" on a card means saved, and an admin can answer "did that land" without the parent reaching the last screen. `holdsUntilVerified()` in `lib/submit.ts` is the entire switch, and it is read by the profile screen, the chat and the completion screen, so the two shapes cannot drift. **The deferred path is still there and is now the fallback**, reached by exactly two sessions: one where no code could be sent (`sendable: false` — production until A2P is approved, where blocking entry would take the tool offline for a carrier decision that has nothing to do with this parent), and one whose confirmation ran out mid-flow — a 401 flips `phone_verified` back to false, the session goes back to holding, and the gate at the end flushes it. **The consequence to accept:** a parent who verifies and then abandons mid-*sharing* leaves a profile row behind, where before they left nothing. The profile itself is safe either way — it is written in the same moment the code is confirmed. That is the price of the honesty, and it changes the answer to "should the review queue see abandoned sessions" (docs/spec-compliance-review.md, open question 8).<br><br>**`SEED_VERIFY_DEV_CODES=1` now works on the server too (14 Aug).** It used to refuse under `NODE_ENV=production` whatever the env said. That guard was right about the risk and wrong about the need: Twilio is unprovisioned until A2P clears, so in production `sendable` was false, every founding parent took the deferred path, and **nothing anyone wrote ever reached the database** — the deployed app could not collect a single row, which is not a state you can run pilot-scale testing in. One switch now, identical behaviour in both places, and `curl <host>/api/seed/verify/status` answers which state a deployment is in.<br><br>**What that costs while it is on, because it is not nothing.** The OTP is untouched — 6 digits, 5 minutes, 3 sends, 3 guesses, 15-minute lock, the whole of §19 exercised exactly as it will be in production. What is gone is *proof of possession*: whoever reads the screen can confirm any number they type. So **invariant 11 stops meaning a real parent stands behind a stored profile**, and a `consents` row records permission for a number nobody proved they hold — and `/admin/consents` is the A2P §3.3 defence file, which a TCPA complaint tests against a phone number. The screen says "QA mode … Real parents never see this" throughout, so the bypass is never hidden. **It comes out before the first real founding contributor, on the same deadline as the `pando` starter password** — and the rows created under it should be cleared or marked `is_test` at the same time, because none of their numbers were verified in the sense the column claims. (`SEED_REQUIRE_VERIFICATION=0` remains the other lever: store everything, leave `phone_verified_at` null, no Founding until a real code lands. It skips the OTP entirely rather than faking it, so it tests less.) |
| **The consent wording in `consent.ts` is the one to register — not the one in the documents** (12 Aug) | The QC Answers doc and spec §19.1 both quote a short standard sentence; `lib/consent.ts` holds the longer one the client dictated on the kickoff call, which names Pando Systems, Inc. and the kinds of message. Put to QuitCode and settled: **ours stands, and the A2P campaign's opt-in description has to carry it verbatim.** §3.7 says the thing that must match is registered-vs-built, so "fixing" this by pasting the spec's sentence into `consent.ts` would create the mismatch it appears to remove. Bump `SMS_CONSENT_TEXT_VERSION` if a word ever changes; never edit a version in place. |
| **Registered SMS copy is verbatim and versioned** (3 Aug) | `lib/sms-templates.ts`. The verification text is A2P sample #3; the client said "production must match". Rewording it means re-registering the sample. Carrier-console settings live in that file's header — opt-in keywords **START and UNSTOP only** (YES removed, because "yes" is an answer to a Network Ask). |
| **One SMS send layer, inert until provisioned** (3 Aug) | `lib/server/sms.ts` is the only caller of Twilio, always through the Messaging Service SID, never a bare number. Unprovisioned ⇒ `{sent:false, reason:"not_provisioned"}` and the UI says text verification isn't switched on yet — the same honesty rule as `persisted:false`. |
| **Single-select chips behave like radios** (3 Aug) | Tapping the chosen chip keeps it chosen. Deselecting cleared the pre-set monthly allowance on the first tap, which is the opposite of what the tap means. |
| **A caregiver card can be held, not dropped** (3 Aug) | "Wouldn't hire again" and any private note keep the card but flag it for a human, and the parent is told so on the spot. The hold is re-derived in the route from the answers — the client can add one, never remove one. |
| **Pay band and pay-benchmark consent are two decisions** (3 Aug) | Client asked for a separate checkbox. Unanswered = no. `pay_benchmark_consent` is a boolean the route sets; the band is stored either way for admin context. |
| Admin is **desktop-first** (the only surface that is) | It's a tool for one or two people at a laptop. It still must work on a phone: nav and tables scroll inside themselves, never the page. The nav row is the one thing here that gets thumbed, so it is 44px on a phone (`min-h-11 md:min-h-10`) — the rest of the admin's denser 36px register is for a cursor. |
| **The admin nav is a worklist, not a menu** (10 Aug) | An admin arrives asking "is there anything for me", and answering it used to mean opening all nine pages. Each queue now carries its own length in the sidebar, fetched once per page load — the shell lives in the layout, so it survives navigation — and re-read on route change, because approving something is precisely what changes the number. Gold badge for pending, **one** red for what is owed a person today (`demand.high_stakes`, `quality.escalations`). `escalations` exists as its own count for that reason: `open_flags` includes `note`-severity ones, so painting the section red on any open flag would make red mean "there are flags", which the number already says. The nine links are grouped (People · Waiting on you · Records), the `hint` that had been in `NAV` since it was written but never rendered now shows for the current page, and the sidebar is `md:sticky` because the contributors table is longer than the viewport. |
| **`alert` is the only red, and it is not a brand colour** (10 Aug) | Added to the theme because `components/admin/*` had `#8a2f2f` / `#fbeeee` / `#d8b4b4` copy-pasted through five files, which the design system forbids — and the cost was exactly what the rule predicts: the nav's urgent badge could not reuse them. Gold means "pending, not finished"; `alert` is reserved for what is owed a person *today*. Two of them and neither means anything. |
| **A claim about a named person is its own D1 class, quieter than high-stakes** (11 Aug) | The Product Strategy's safety table gives "negative claims about a named nanny, doctor, teacher or parent" their own row and their own answer: *human review only; never broadly circulated or automatically written into the knowledge base*. So `named_allegation` sits **above** `high_stakes` in `classifyDemand`'s ladder rather than inside it — the two are owed different things. A safety question is owed professional resources now; an allegation is owed *silence* until a person has read it, which means no resource list, no reassurance that reads as agreement, and nothing that repeats the claim back. The classifier needs **two** signals (an accusation *and* a person cue) so "the class was cancelled and they lied about the refund" doesn't trip it, and `demand_signals_allegation_review_check` holds the storage half in the database, so no future caller can be the one that forgets. **Do not fold this back into `high_stakes` to "simplify the enum"** — it would attach a resource list to an accusation. |
| **`answer_ready` lives on the place, and can only sit on an approved one** (11 Aug) | §17.1's golden-answer flag: an admin marks the records already complete enough to power an excellent answer with no Blast behind them. On `shares` rather than `share_contributions` because the thing that answers a question is the venue or class, not one parent's sentence about it. The `CHECK` is the point of the column — "ready to answer with" must be a subset of "reviewed by a human", or the flag becomes a way to route unreviewed parent text into an answer, which is invariant 8 with extra steps. The write is a **conditional UPDATE, not a validation**: a stale admin screen would otherwise abort the whole transaction, audit row included, and a no-op is the right answer to "mark a record ready that has since been rejected". |
| **Camps are their own taxonomy category, not a kind of class** (11 Aug) | v3.2 §8.4/§15.3 added them, and the reason is calendar-shaped: camp registration (Jan–Mar in LA) is the highest-intent parenting search of the year, and the pilot ends in December — the dataset has to exist *before* the season it answers. A camp is also not a recurring class, so a parent scanning that chip list for last summer's camp does not find it there. It carries the same affinity as a class (§7.1, "same regular activity or class", weight 4): two families at the same camp week overlap in exactly the way that signal means. Added as a sixth group on the existing "Your circles" screen — **not a new screen**, so the July question set's order is untouched. `focus` remains the one §15.3 category with no chips: it is what a parent will be *asked* about, and the questionnaire asks that as static topic taps (P11/P12). |
| **The demand signal's neighborhood is read from the profile, never from the body** (11 Aug) | Same rule as the affinity graph, for the same reason: §9 and QC Answers Q7 make this number the market-expansion signal — which area Pando opens next — so the browser does not get a vote. `writeCompletion` selects it from `people` inside the insert. Nullable on purpose: the anonymous path has no `people` row, and an invented neighborhood would corrupt the one number the column exists to produce. |
| **The consent file shows the phone number unmasked — that is the feature** (11 Aug) | Every other admin surface masks it because nobody needs it to make a decision. `/admin/consents` is the A2P §3.3 defence file, and a defence file that cannot say *which number* agreed proves nothing. For the same reason test rows are shown and labelled rather than filtered: a TCPA complaint arrives about a phone number, not about our idea of which rows count. The download is built from rows already on screen so that reading it writes the audit row that says who took a list of numbers, and when. **"Fixing the leak" by masking this one page destroys the only artefact that answers a complaint.** |
| **Reference data arrives by script with a dry run, never by upload button** (11 Aug) | `npm run options:import -- sheet.csv` (QC Answers Q10: Janet supplies a Google Sheet with `market_id, category, option_value, active`). It replaces the reference data the whole questionnaire is built from, so it is a deliberate act with a diff: it prints what would change and needs `--commit` before writing. It will not invent a category — only the eight §15.3 names are accepted, so a typo cannot create `nieghborhoods` and silently empty a screen — and it deletes nothing: `--retire-missing` sets `active = false`, because a parent may already have selected a value and `market_options` is what their stored answer resolves against. Since 12 Aug the questionnaire **reads that table at runtime** (see the row below), so an import changes what the next parent sees without a deploy — the importer's own writes wait out the 60s read cache, because it runs in a different process. |
| **The tap lists are read from the database, and `lib/market-options.ts` is the fallback** (12 Aug) | Reverses the halfway state that made `market_options` a table the app wrote to and never read: chips were compiled into the client bundle, so a promoted "other" answer and an imported sheet both did nothing until someone edited a TS file and deployed — while `/admin/options` claimed the chip was already there. Now `GET /api/market/options` serves the table, `useMarketOptions` loads it once per session, and `marketOptions()` prefers it (spec §8.5, §16.2). **Three rules that make it safe, and none is optional:** an *empty* category falls back to the built-in list, because "nobody seeded it" is likelier than "this market offers nothing" and a screen with no options is a dead end a parent cannot report; a failed fetch is silent and keeps the built-in lists, same rule as `persisted: false`; and the server never populates the runtime table, so there is no request-scoped state to leak between users. The read is cached 60s in `lib/server/market-cache.ts` and **cleared after any `option.*` admin write — after the commit, never before**, or a racing read repopulates it with pre-write rows for the full minute. |
| **Promoting an "other" answer repairs the graph of whoever typed it** (12 Aug) | Invariant 9 parks a free-text answer as unmatchable, which means the parent who wrote it never got the affinity edge that everyone tapping the canonical chip got — and nothing was putting it back, so promotion quietly created a permanent hole in the matching graph. `option.promote` now also approves every *pending duplicate of the same value* (two parents naming one club is one judgement, not two) and writes the affinity or relevance row for each of their submitters. **The value stored is the admin's slug, never the parent's free text** — matching keys on the slug, so storing what they typed would recreate the unmatchable state promotion exists to end. `person_schools` is deliberately not written for a promoted school: that table carries a per-school status nobody was asked for, and inventing "current" would put a fact in the record that no parent stated. This is a re-derivation, not a rewrite: `raw_answers` still says exactly what they tapped. **Watch the drizzle trap** — the id list is built as a Postgres array *literal*, because `sql` expands a JS array into a record and `unnest` then fails, the same way it did in `repo/caregiver.ts`. |
| **The OTP follows spec §19, and its lock is keyed to the phone** (12 Aug) | The numbers were 10 minutes, 3 sends and 5 wrong guesses with nothing after them; §19 says *"OTPs expire after 5 minutes; max 3 attempts then 15-minute lock"*. The old shape was friendlier and weaker in a way that is easy to miss: burning the code on the last guess still left two more sends, so one session could put **fifteen** guesses against a six-digit code. The lock closes that, and it is stored **per number, not per pending verification** — on the verification it would be worth nothing, because clearing a cookie asks for a fresh one. Consequences to keep: `startVerification` checks the lock *before* the hourly send cap, so a locked number cannot buy its way out with a new code; the confirm screen switches to the blocked state rather than saying "send a fresh one", which would be advice that cannot work; and none of these numbers appears in the message, so changing them never touches the registered copy. Asserted in `npm run test:e2e` from a second browser session, which is the case a per-session counter would pass and this must not. |
| **"Text DELETE and the whole profile goes" is a real delete** (12 Aug) | It is the last sentence of the caregiver flow, so a soft-delete would make the app lie on the one screen where a person is deciding to trust it. `claim.delete` removes the claim, the copied `caregiver_profiles` row, their caregiver-scope consents and the `people` row they created — one statement, so nothing can half-happen — and takes a linked `caregivers` row back to `revoked` with every visibility flag off. **Two boundaries that are the whole design:** the `people` row survives if anything else is attached to it (submissions, a nomination, a non-caregiver consent), because a delete request from one role must not wipe the other; and the nominating parent's card stays, because it is that parent's own contribution and holds no way to contact anybody (invariant 13) — which is what makes "the whole profile" and "everything Pando has about you" the same thing here. The action demands *how they asked* and never asks why, per the same promise. |

| **The floor is five questions a month, not one — and the numbers live in three places that must agree** (18 Aug) | The 8.18 strategy round set Community Access at **five** questions a month, so `ALLOWANCE` is now `5 / 10 / as_relevant` with **5 as the default**. Read the wording carefully before "restoring" a lower option: five is the network's actual floor for free access, not a cautious opening offer, so it is the default rather than a step up from one. **The expensive part was not the option list.** The old 1/3/5 scheme was enforced in *two* places, and widening only the first is what made this a migration rather than an edit: `/api/seed/profile` was updated to accept `[5, 10]`, the `allowance_shape` CHECK still said `IN (1, 3, 5)`, and so a value the route had just called valid aborted the write one layer down. Found by `test:e2e` asserting the *landed* value rather than the 200 — a suite that stopped at the response would have passed. `drizzle/0013` moves existing rows off the old default (3 → 5) **before** adding the constraint, or the ADD aborts on the first row that violates it. **Three places, and all three have to say the same thing:** `ALLOWANCE` in `lib/questions.ts` (what a parent can tap), the route's `[5, 10]` (what the server accepts), and the CHECK (what the database allows). `as_relevant` remains the one option that stores `null` — the shape the CHECK exists to enforce. |
| **A listening ear is its own consent, not a part of the allowance answer** (18 Aug) | The 8.18 round added a question after the allowance screen: some parents ask Pando the things they cannot ask anywhere else, and would you occasionally answer one. It is a **separate `consents` scope** (`listening_ear`, drizzle `0012`) rather than a flag on the person, for the same reason follow-up, Blast and reference are three records and not one: it is a different amount of exposure, and a consent that cannot be revoked on its own is not really a consent. Two rules the copy makes promises about, so neither is optional: the answer is **anonymous** to the asker — the screen says "it's never who they think" — and it **spends the same monthly allowance as everything else**, so saying yes is not a second, hidden budget. Options are `opted_in` / `declined`; there is deliberately no third state, because an unanswered question is already "no" everywhere else in this flow. |
| **An admin's follow-up question is stored, because the screen promises it is** (18 Aug) | `contribution.needs_detail` flipped the status and threw the question away — it survived only inside `audit_log.after`, where nobody working the review queue would think to look. Meanwhile the button's own copy said the card "stays in the queue until they answer", which is a promise that the question was remembered. `share_contributions.needs_detail_note` (drizzle `0011`) is what makes that sentence true. Same shape as `confidence_note` and for the same reason: a state that exists only as a side effect of an audit row is a state nobody can act on. **There is no channel to send it yet** — that is Phase 2 — so this is the record of what will be asked, not evidence it was. |
| **A referral is capped at three, counted at write time** (18 Aug) | `referral.link` had no ceiling, so one contributor could be credited with an unbounded number of referrals — and since a credit is denominated in Network Asks (which do not exist yet), an uncapped count is a promise of a balance nothing can spend. The cap is **three**, enforced by counting rows in the same transaction as the insert, and a fourth returns `referral_cap_reached` rather than silently succeeding. **Deliberately not a schema constraint:** three rows are countable at write time, a CHECK cannot express "per referrer", and a trigger would put the rule somewhere an admin reading `admin-write.ts` would not find it. |
| **The admin reads in English, not in ids — and `labels.ts` is the only place that translation lives** (19 Aug) | The client's verdict on the admin was that it was written for whoever built it: `possible_named_person`, `stale_at_capture`, `share_contribution`, `pending_review`, price bands rendered as `mid_range`. Every one of those is a stored value, and the page was printing the storage. `lib/admin/labels.ts` is now the single map from stored id → sentence a non-technical reader can act on, and it is deliberately **not** a `slugLabel` that title-cases the id: "Possible Named Person" is the same jargon with capitals. Two rules that keep it from drifting. (1) *One file, not per-page constants* — the same `review_status` appears on four pages, and four copies is how three of them end up out of date; `optionLabel(OPTIONS, value)` next to `slugLabel` is for the case where a real option list already holds the label. (2) *`flagTitle` and `flagMeaning` are separate* — the title names the flag, the meaning explains that *kind* of flag, and a card shows the specific stored reason **or** the generic meaning, never both. Printing both is what made the Flags page unreadable: twelve cards each explaining themselves twice. |
| **Every stored value on an admin screen goes through `labels.ts` — and the same value must read the same on every page** (20 Aug) | The second half of the 19 Aug rewrite, and it found more than wording. Eight surfaces were still printing storage: `sensitivity` and `status` on the demand queue, `recommendation` and `freshness_state` on contributions, `consent_evidence.method` and the caregiver `type`, the audit log's `action` and `resource`, and `firsthand` / `secondhand` — while a *filter tab on the same page* already said "Heard from a friend". **The rule that came out of it:** a value gets its words in `labels.ts` (or from the option list it was chosen from, via `optionLabel`), and no page writes its own. The overview was calling `peer_support` "Wanting to hear from others" while the demand queue called it something else, and `invited` was "Sent an invite" against "Family sent the invite" — one thing under two names, on two pages an admin sees minutes apart. Both now read the shared map. **Three labelling traps worth keeping in mind.** (1) *A label must not collide with the column beside it* — `high_stakes` was "Health, legal or safety", which is also the name of the D1 *category*, so two adjacent columns said the same words and neither distinguished the row; it is "Needs a professional" now, which answers what the column is for. (2) *A heading has to stand alone* — "Counts towards it" had no antecedent, since "it" was whichever of the next two columns you were looking at. (3) *A word before the number* — the usefulness score was a bare `38%` on contributions and a labelled band on Flags; one scale, one presentation, both places. Verified page by page in a browser against the demo cohort, not by grepping. |
| **A label that describes a state nothing maintains is a bug, not wording** (20 Aug) | Three came out of the pass above, and each was a sentence the system could not keep. **(a)** The demand queue printed "not usable until read" from `requires_human_review` — which is set once at capture and **nothing ever clears**, so a question you had read, followed up and answered still claimed to be unread, permanently. It is now tied to what does change (`status === "open"`) and says "waiting for you to read it". **(b)** The founding queue's "Invited by: …" read `people.invited_via_group`, which names a *group* and never a person: the "who invited you?" question was removed on 12 Aug, so the label promised a fact the app no longer collects and its empty state blamed a parent for skipping a question that no longer exists. Corrected, it turned out to be redundant — those cards are already grouped by the link somebody arrived on — so the line went and only the "no group recorded" badge stayed, which is the one thing the heading cannot say. **(c)** The caregiver row rendered "with Prefer not to say" as a benefit and "rate is for your eyes only" beside a rate the family had declined to give; both are `exclusive` refusals in their option lists and now suppress the clause instead of decorating it. **The general rule:** when a screen states a condition, find the write that maintains it. If there isn't one, either build it or change the sentence — never leave the claim standing. |
| **An admin arrives asking "is there anything for me", so the overview answers that and nothing else** (19 Aug) | The page had been a grid of every count the system can produce, which is a different question — "what are the numbers" — and it buried the two or three things actually waiting. It now leads with a **worklist built from the queues that are non-empty**, urgent first, and each row links to the page that clears it. **The trap, and it caught me twice:** `quality.open_flags` *includes* `quality.escalations`, so listing both as separate rows double-counts — the read queue is `open_flags − escalations`. And the second-order version of the same mistake: after splitting them, two rows pointed at different pages while the sidebar counted one number, so the two surfaces disagreed by two. Any new row here has to be checked against what the sidebar already counts, because they read the same `overview` payload and a user sees both at once. |
| **One concept, one button — and a held card must still be approvable** (19 Aug) | The contributions queue showed one button on some rows and two on others, with no rule a reader could infer. The cause was a real bug rather than a style drift: the approve action was gated on `status === "pending_review"`, so a card that had been put on hold or sent back for detail had **no way forward at all** — the queue could accumulate cards nobody could act on. It is now `status !== "approved"`, i.e. the action is offered whenever it would change something, and the status is a pill rather than a third button. Same rule applied to the wording: a button says what happens ("Add to Pando", "I've read it"), and its tooltip says what else it does (saves the comment, takes it off the list) — "Done" and "Submit" were describing the click, not the consequence. |
| **A nav hint is worth having before you click, and never after** (19 Aug) | `NAV` had carried a one-line `hint` per page since it was written; 10 Aug started rendering it for the current page. It read as helpful and was pure duplication — every page has a `PageHead` intro saying the same thing, so the sidebar and the page said it twice, side by side, on all nine pages. The hints now live on the nav link's `title` instead, where the reader has not yet arrived and the page's own intro has not answered them. **The general rule this is an instance of:** the admin's problem was never missing explanation, it was the same explanation in three places — a note under every input, a paragraph under every card, and a footnote raising an open question *for the client* under Janet's own queue. Say it once, at the top, or in the tooltip of the control it belongs to. |
| **The demo cohort is ordinary data with a marker, not `is_test` data** (19 Aug) | `npm run seed:demo` fills the database with a realistic Pasadena founding cohort — 24 contributors, 21 shares, 10 caregivers across the ladder, claims, demand signals, flags, referrals — so the client can be walked through every admin surface without meeting an empty state. **It is deliberately not `is_test`:** every admin count filters test rows out, so a cohort marked that way would render a dashboard of zeros, which is the opposite of the point. The marker is `people.source = 'demo'` — a real column with real values, which renders as "Arrived via: demo" on the contributor page rather than hiding what it is, and which is what `--clear` walks outward from. Everything the script creates hangs off one of those people, so removing them by that marker takes the whole cohort and touches nothing a real parent made (verified: 24 removed, three real people and a hand-made card left standing). Phones are `626-555-0XXX` — the 555 fiction convention, with the `0` block chosen to stay clear of `test:e2e`'s generated numbers. `npm run check` passes against it, which is the actual assurance: the invariants are enforced rather than merely asserted. |
| **1.8's confirm-back is asked on vagueness, not on a score — and the field names are the chat's, not the database's** (26 Aug) | Estimate 1.8 asks for *"a short confirm-back question when confidence is low or an answer is vague"*, and it had never been built: the extraction pass scored text and flagged it for an admin, which is the *low-confidence* half arriving too late to help. The score does not exist when it would be useful — extraction runs after the save response and deliberately does not block the parent (6 Aug) — so this implements the **vagueness** half, which needs no model: "good", "it's fine", "we loved it" are thin by length and by the absence of anything specific, measurable for free before the card is even sent. Asked **before** persisting, so the card is written once with the fuller answer rather than saved thin and patched. **Four rules it will not break:** never twice for one card; never on a caregiver card (its free text *is* the restricted note, invariant 12); never on a field the parent skipped, because skipping is an answer; and "It's fine as it is" is as reachable as the input, because a follow-up you cannot decline is a required field wearing a friendly hat. **The bug worth remembering:** the first version listed `tip_text`, `what_makes_it_great` — the **database column names**. The chat's submission fields are **script step ids**, where the tip is `tip` (`repo/cards.ts` maps one to the other). So nothing ever matched, the feature silently never fired, and `test:confirm` passed — because the test had been written against the same wrong names. Found only by walking it in a browser and reading what localStorage actually held. Same shape as the `bands` bug: typecheck clean, tests green, feature dead. **And the marker is stripped twice.** `__confirm_back_asked` has to survive a reload, so it lives on the card in local storage; the chat strips `__`-prefixed keys before sending, and `/api/seed/save` strips them again — not a duplicate, because `submissions.fields` is the verbatim record of what the parent typed, and an internal marker in it is a small lie about what they said. |
| **Estimate 2.2's two dead numbers and its empty funnel** (26 Aug) | An audit of the estimate against the app — not against its status column, which says "In progress" for every M1 row and nothing at all for M2 — turned up three things that were built halfway. (1) `consent.follow_up_opt_in` and `consent.reference_willing` were queried, serialised and sent to the overview **for weeks without being rendered**: dead payload on the one screen whose job is to say where the pilot stands, and named in the estimate as "reference-willing and opt-in counts". (2) `drop_off` was a hardcoded `[]` under a comment saying funnels live in PostHog. Half right and wholly unhelpful: the page already had the component to draw it, so the block never appeared and nobody could tell whether that meant "no drop-off" or "not built" — and the admin has to work with PostHog unconfigured, the same honesty rule as `persisted: false`. It is now four steps built from **stored rows** rather than client events, so it survives an ad-blocker. (3) The per-link funnel the estimate asks for ("opens vs completions per channel") had completions and no denominator: `/admin/invites` could say a group brought four contributors but never whether that was four out of six or four out of two hundred. `invites.opens` (drizzle `0016`) is incremented from the `/join` server render — **not** from `validateInviteCode`, which runs on every card save and would have made "opens" mean "requests". It counts opens and not people, deliberately: de-duplicating needs an identifier before consent, and nothing about a person may be stored before their number is verified (invariant 11). The inflation is roughly uniform across channels, which is what keeps the comparison usable, and the column comment says so rather than letting a future reader mistake it for a headcount. |
| **Sharing a connection is one decision per connection, and dropping one is a revocation with a timestamp** (24 Aug) | Item 18's second half — her Privacy Guidance §A. Until now "may Pando mention a shared connection" was one answer per person; her model is one per *connection*, because the exposure differs: a parent at a 900-pupil school and a parent at a 40-member club risk very different amounts of identifiability from the same sentence. `affiliation_visibility` (drizzle `0015`) is **the only derived table in the profile write that is not deleted and rewritten**, and that is the whole design. Everything else there is a computed fact, so re-deriving is free; this is a recorded consent carrying a wording version and the moment it was given, and §I asks that an attributed statement be reconstructable from its supporting records — rewriting the row would reset `consented_at` to whenever the parent last edited anything. So three cases: newly granted inserts stamped now; **still granted is left entirely alone**, not even a touched timestamp, because re-confirming is not a new decision; and **a grant that disappears from the payload is a revocation** — back to `private` with `revoked_at`, per §G's "record the effective time of the change". The row is kept, never deleted: the audit question is "what was allowed, when". **Four things worth not undoing.** (1) *Nothing is pre-selected and the screen is skippable* — §A says "Continue is not consent", so absence from the list *is* the private default and no "none of them" option is needed. (2) *The screen is skipped entirely when the master switch is off*, because a parent who said "don't mention my connections" has already answered it. (3) *Identity sharing is not a value in `visibility`* — §A calls it "never persistent; requires separate permission for each introduction", and storing it as a state would make a per-introduction decision look standing. (4) *The grant is stored in the graph's vocabulary* (`school`, `social_group`), not the questionnaire's (`schools`, `clubs`) — `lib/affiliations.ts` owns that map precisely so a second copy on the server cannot file a permission against an edge nothing reads. **Two traps found while building it.** `cleanId` refuses a colon, so routing `schools:walden-school` through the default sanitiser dropped **every** grant silently — hence `cleanAffiliationRef`, its own shape check rather than a looser one for every id in the app. And typed connections are deliberately **not** offered: unmatchable until promoted (invariant 9), so the toggle would be a decision with no effect and a row that could never be joined. **What is not built:** §§B–F and §H — eligibility, counting by household, the 1/2/3+ wording, the no-combining rule, the introduction flow. All of those are the answering path, and Phase 1 has none. This table is the record they will read. |
| **Where a parent lived before is a canonical place, and the geography lives in the id's *prefix*** (24 Aug) | Item 11 split one screen into three signals: current tenure (her bands, 4–9 not 3–10), **local roots** and **previous places**. "I grew up here" came out of the tenure list, and that is the substance rather than a tidy-up — as an option there it was mutually exclusive with every band, so a parent who grew up in Pasadena, moved away and came back had to pick one truth and drop the other. And "Where did you move from?" is **gone as a question**: it was asked only of parents under three years here, so a family who moved from London twelve years ago had nowhere to say so, and it asked for a coarse band Pando can compute. Her instruction verbatim — *"Pando can derive 'elsewhere in California,' 'another state' or 'another country' from the actual location. The parent shouldn't have to provide both."* **The design trap, and it is worth reading before touching `lib/places.ts`.** The classification has to be readable from the id alone, because `deriveLifeRelevance` is pure and runs over sanitised answers with no access to the options table — that is the 11 Aug rule that the graph is derived and never taken from the request. The first attempt put the geography in the id's **suffix** (`london-uk`, `san-francisco-ca`) and `scripts/seed-places.mjs`'s own check refused the batch: country codes collide with US state codes across the board — **DE** is Germany and Delaware, **IN** India and Indiana, **IL** Israel and Illinois, **MA** Morocco and Massachusetts, **AR** Argentina and Arkansas, **ID** Indonesia and Idaho, **CA** Canada and California, **CO** Colombia and Colorado. Twelve seeded cities would have filed a Berlin family as living in another US state, and nothing in the code would have looked wrong. The ambiguity is in the vocabulary, so no special-casing fixes it — hence `us-` / `intl-`, a prefix we own. `npm run test:derive` (21 checks) holds every collision in **both** directions, plus that three Californian cities are one signal and not three, and that a place a parent typed contributes nothing until an admin promotes it (invariant 9). **The question is search-only**: `previous_places` is the one category with no starter set, because there is no plausible list of 8–12 familiar *previous* cities — and missing it from the endpoint's directory list for one build served all 182 and showed "Abu Dhabi, Accra, Albuquerque" as suggestions. |
| **Which link a parent arrived on is a super-property, not an event prop** (27 Aug) | Estimate 3.1 asks for the funnel to be *"segmented by which shared link the parent arrived from"*, and 2.2 for drop-off as a per-link funnel. Found by auditing the app against the 3.2-updated estimate: **not one of the 49 `track()` calls carried the invite code.** `seed_link_opened` sent `source`, which is `qr` vs `link` — the *channel*, never *which group* — and every event after it carried nothing about the arrival at all. A PostHog funnel can only break down by a property present on its steps, so "which link actually brought contributors" was answerable in our own admin (`invites.opens` plus the completion counts, 26 Aug) and **nowhere in PostHog**, which is where the estimate puts it. `identifyArrival` uses `posthog.register`, so the values ride on every later capture — and that is the point rather than a detail: the drop-off worth measuring happens three screens after the link was opened, and a prop on `seed_link_opened` alone would segment exactly one step of the funnel. **Three things worth keeping.** (1) *It is called before the first `track`*, in the same effect, so `seed_link_opened` carries it too. (2) *The group is the slug* (`invites.group_option_value`), not `group_label` — matching and grouping key on slugs everywhere else in this app, and a display name breaks the day somebody edits a spelling. (3) *Safe under invariant 7, and that is why it is allowed at all*: an invite code is one per **group**, never per parent (12 Aug), so `mops-altadena` is an enum and not an identifier — nothing registered here is a name, a number or free text. A typed code re-registers, or every event after a manual entry would be attributed to the empty link the parent landed on. |
| **A neighborhood the parent typed is still an answer** (27 Aug) | Found from a client's session recording, and it had cost them a **completed** profile. P3 sets `allowOther`, and `isQuestionAnswered` counts a typed entry — so the screen let them past, the review screen printed what they wrote, and the flow told them it was saved. `/api/seed/profile` then asked `cleanId` for a canonical id, got null, and refused the **entire profile** with *"Neighborhood and child age are required"* — for a question they had plainly answered. They verified their phone with a correct code (`Seed_verify_confirmed` fired, the 422 came from the profile write immediately after) and watched three saves fail, with nothing on screen that could suggest going back to tap a listed area. **The rule now:** a typed answer *is* an answer for §8.5's two required questions; what it is not is *matchable*, and that was already handled without this route's help — `derivePendingOptions` files the words under `neighborhoods` for `/admin/options`, `people.neighborhood` is nullable and **stays null** rather than holding words no taxonomy contains, and promoting it writes the affinity row for everyone who typed it (12 Aug). So invariant 9 holds and the hole closes itself. Only the presence check was relaxed: whitespace and a genuine absence are still refused. `repo/profile.ts` took `neighborhood: string` because the route used to guarantee an id; it is `string | null` now. **Two things this also fixed.** (1) *The 422 named neither field.* One message for two failures is what made it undiagnosable — it now says which of the two, with a `fields` array, so the next one is readable from a network tab. (2) *`normaliseAnswers` guarded shape and not domain.* It kept any finite number in `child_ages` while `cleanAges` takes -1..25, so a stored value outside that range would survive every reload and be refused every time. Dropping it leaves the required question visibly unanswered and `canAdvance` blocks — the parent is asked, rather than handed a save that cannot work. **The trap for whoever reads this next:** I first assumed the stored value was a birth year and built a `2025 → 1` recovery for it. The git history disproved it — the option ids have been ages since the first commit, so no build ever wrote a year — and the recovery came back out. The real cause was reachable only from *how the parent answered*, not from the value's shape. |
| **The starter chips are filtered to the parent's area, and the area is matched on a slug** (27 Aug) | Found by the client walking the flow: *"дуже багато опцій, важко шукати, тапати"*. Two separate faults under one symptom. **(1) The comparison was against a display name.** `market_options.area` holds the client's wording ("La Cañada Flintridge"); the parent's answer is the neighborhood option id ("la-canada-flintridge"). Both the chip list and the search endpoint compared them with `lower()`, which bridges a single-word name and **nothing else** — so Altadena, Arcadia, Glendale and five others ranked by accident while **La Cañada Flintridge, Highland Park, South Pasadena, Sierra Madre, Monterey Park, San Marino, San Gabriel, Eagle Rock and Temple City did not**. Nine of seventeen areas: a La Cañada parent was shown twelve schools in alphabetical order, not one in their own city, by code that had run and found no matches. Same shape as the `bands` bug — typecheck clean, tests green, feature dead. `area_slug` (drizzle `0017`) is the fix, written by `taxonomy:import` from the same `slug()` that already folds `La Cañada` → `la-canada`; the SQL backfill was **verified equal to the JS for all 231 distinct areas** before being trusted. Matching keys on a slug and never on display text is the rule everywhere else in this app (the promoted-option rule, the previous-places prefix) — comparing against a label breaks silently the day somebody edits a spelling. **(2) Ranking alone was never enough.** Her sheets curate **exactly eight school starters per area**, so a cap of twelve gave a parent their own eight plus four from wherever sorted first — not familiar choices, just the top of a list. The starters are now **filtered** to the parent's area. **This does not narrow what can be picked:** search still covers all 357 across the market and the footnote says so, and the "never a filter" rule is about *eligibility* — a closed school stays selectable, the next town stays reachable. What is filtered is which twelve are offered as taps. **The floor is the part not to remove:** schools are eight per area but baby activities run from eleven in Pasadena down to **two** in Altadena and clubs to zero, so below `AREA_FLOOR = 8` the list tops up — ordered by **how many starters each other area has**, which puts Pasadena first (the market's centre, where families in the thin areas actually go) rather than Alhambra first for no perceptible reason. Verified per directory against the live table: schools 8 own-area, activities 2 + 6 Pasadena, faith 1 + 7, clubs 0 + 8. **The test that would have caught it now exists:** `test:e2e` asserted only that the home area *never filters* — the half that worked — so it passed throughout. It now also asserts that a **multi-word** area ranks its own records first, because a single-word one passes either way. |
| **Tap first, search second — and a searched record has to survive a reload** (24 Aug) | Item 7's UI. `SearchableChipGroup` wraps `ChipGroup` rather than replacing it: selection semantics, the exclusive rule, the cap and its hint and the typed "other" answers all already work and are tested, so the only new thing is *where an option can come from*. The component's whole job is to widen the `options` array it hands down. **Three rules, and the third is the one that took a real bug to find.** (1) *Starters are ranked and capped at twelve* — `/options` serves every curated starter in the market (133 schools across 17 areas, because the server does not know which area this parent picked), and rendering all of them is the failure the feature exists to fix, one level down.
